### Chapter 2. On the Causes Which Ought to Move and Impel Us to the Study of the Divine Word⁶²

**The saying of Hugh is celebrated,** worthy of memory: ***"Nothing in this life is felt more sweet, nothing taken up more eagerly, nothing so strengthens the soul against temptations, nothing so awakens and helps the man to every work and to every labour — as the study of Sacred Scripture, which is also the Mistress of the other sciences, which are as it were her handmaids."***⁶³

Let us now have in view the ***causes which ought to move us to the study of sacred doctrine.***

**The first is the strict command of God,** as these and similar places testify: **Exod. 13**: *"Let the law of the Lord always be in thy mouth."* **Deut. 31**: ***"Read the words of this law before all Israel in their hearing, and let all the people be gathered together in one — men, women, little ones, and the stranger that is within thy gates — that hearing they may learn and fear the Lord your God."*** **Joshua 1**: ***"Let not the volume of this law depart out of thy mouth, but thou shalt meditate therein by day and by night."*** **Isaiah 8**: ***"To the law and to the testimony."*** **John 5**: ***"Search the Scriptures, for they testify of me"*** — that is, ***read diligently and weigh the words and judgements in the writings;*** ***repeat what you read,*** for the Bible read pleases, and re-read pleases still more; ***confer with others,*** interrogate, hear — for it is an old saying: ***"He is a bad teacher who does not deign to hear the disciple."*** ***Item:*** *"Everyone is his own worst master."*⁶⁴

Item: *"To wish to learn nothing whence learning is offered, is impious."* And ***Nazianzen*** reprehends theologians who have not heard suitable doctors, but suddenly are born — as ***earth-born Titan-blooded Giants wage war on heaven.*** There must therefore be ***μελέτη or spoudē*** — meditation and study — in every study, especially in the study of Sacred Letters, of which Christ says ***"Search"*** [John 5:39], and Paul (1 Tim. 4:13): ***"Attend to reading, to exhortation, and to doctrine."***⁶⁵

Hither pertains the celebrated sentence of ***Epiphanius,*** which stands in Book II, Volume I, against the *Apotactici* or *Apostolici*, similar to our Franciscan monks: ***"The divine Words do not need allegories, but their native meaning is to be retained,"*** — namely in the moral law, in the promise of grace and eternal salvation, in the articles of faith, in the sacraments, in the Symbols, and elsewhere, where anything is recited in proper and perspicuous speech. But ***speculation*** is needed — that is (to use Chrysostom's words) — ***"that we, having driven off every secular care, may compel our thoughts, and restrain our mind lest it be distracted, and give ourselves to reading with great piety and attention, that we may be led by the Holy Spirit to the understanding of Scripture, considering the context of speech, the members, the order, the force of words and judgements — every apex, every point,"*** as in the little verses it is said:
> ***"Nothing so small is that hidden lies from thee,***
> ***And often one point holds a great relief."***⁶⁶

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### Chapter 2 (continued). The Remaining Causes — Sensus, Traditio, and Seven Motives to Scripture-Study

**Then there is need of *sensus*** — that is, of the ***movement and efficacy of the Holy Spirit,*** *polishing and helping* the study and speculation and *producing them into act and into the palaestra itself* — so that the studious one may, in the very deed, know and experience ***what it is that he has read*** — what is anguish, the sense of sin and of God's wrath, death — what is consolation, faith, life. For without this ***sensus,*** the Scripture is called a ***bare and mute letter*** — whence Clement says that the ***Spirit is the soul and virtue of the Word.*** And Chrysostom rightly and piously affirming the same, says: ***"The Divine Scripture has not need of the wisdom of men that it may be understood, but of the revelation of the Spirit, that from it, having been drawn, the true sense may increase to us as gain from the images."*** The same: ***"It cannot happen that one who is exercised in the Divine Scriptures with great study and fervent desire should always be neglected — but the Lord Himself, entering our hearts from above, illumines the mind, and is the Doctor of those things which we do not know."*** — For this sensus is required not ***enthusiasm*** or ***imaginary rapture,*** but — as *D. Luther* was wont to say — ***"oratio, meditatio, tentatio"*** (**prayer, meditation, and trial**), which give understanding, and without which ***no man can say anything solid.*** As it is said in the vulgar proverb today: *"An unsifted theologian is either a babbler, or a hypocrite, or a heretic, or a seducer."***⁶⁷

Then also ***tradition*** must be used, as Epiphanius says — that is, one must know ***what the other Orthodox have handed down, and how the pious and sincere Doctors of the Church have explained and illustrated the Scriptures.*** Their testimonies, sayings, parables, histories, examples congruent with the Scriptures, figures, similitudes, and the rest — must be brought forward. — Now let us come to the other causes.⁶⁸

**The Second Cause** is the ***end of our creation, redemption, and sanctification.*** For why have we been created by God? — namely that we may know, celebrate, and worship God, as also our reason inculcates the same, so that even the ***Gentiles*** — deprived of the light of the divine Word — have said: ***"All things were made for the sake of the man; but the man for the sake of God."*** And why do we live? — because, namely, ***that we may proclaim, exercise, and by use confirm the true doctrine of God, and study to propagate it to posterity.*** For man does not live by bread alone, but by every word which proceeds from the mouth of God (Deut. 8:3). And in Ps. 118 [Vulg.] it is written: ***"I shall not die, but live, and shall declare the works of the Lord."*** For the same cause we are also ***redeemed and sanctified,*** that we may walk before God in truth and holiness — that is, be studious of the Divine Word, and thence obey the will of God when known.⁶⁹

**The Third Cause** is the ***certainty of Christian doctrine,*** or of the Sacred Writings. For ***Sacred Scripture alone*** has been principally given, confirmed, conserved, and approved by God — since other arts and sciences and *σοφίαι* (as Clement speaks) come *by consequence,* not principally, from God, and do not reach ***the target of truth,*** nor see the mathematical point or centre — but, as Aristotle says of the Morals, ***acquiesce in a physical point, or on the surface, provided only they do not depart from the circumference.*** Hence were born those philosophical utterances of Socrates: ***"One thing only I know, that I know nothing."*** ***"Either nothing or little we know."*** ***"The greatest thing of what we know is the least of those things which we do not know."*** And of Aristotle: ***"Human minds fixed on the contemplation of nature are blinded, as bats turned toward the noonday light."***⁷⁰

Clement of Alexandria argues that ***Philosophy is a theft of the Devil*** — or the ***fire of Prometheus stolen from Jove*** — which the Devil transported to men, whence so many Philosophers arose among the Gentiles who sought their counsels openly even from the Devil, as ***the oracles*** testify — and the saying of Socrates in the *Theages* is cited: ***"There is to me a sign of a demon, which from earliest age has by divine allotment befallen me."***

To this Christ is said to allude in John 10: ***"All who came before me were thieves and robbers,"*** — that is, ***who came in place of me and despised me,*** ***κατ' ἐμοῦ,*** ***wished to be superior to me, greater than me, wiser than me,*** — and, as Augustine expounds, ***through pride wished to precede me, spurning me and my doctrine.***

This deceit, violence, and malice of the Devil ***God has wonderfully inverted,*** so that ***philosophical doctrine*** ought now to be the ***handmaid and minister of the Divine Word,*** subserving the glory of God — and, applied in its place, doing much for the illustration of the heavenly doctrine — since it is no longer *νοσηρά και προσποιητός* (diseased and pretended), but serves piety and discipline.⁷¹

But — to return to certainty — the ***whole of Philosophy*** is engaged either in ***uncertain disputations,*** flexible now to this side, now to that, or often to many sides — deviated and mutable, which beget dissensions among various men and various again; or is engaged in ***doubtful doubtings,*** as certain *gymnosophists* said to Alexander — things ***to be weighed*** or, as Cicero speaks, ***matters in which judgement must be reserved,*** which one must ***bear in mind,*** they are ***ἐποχή*** (suspension of judgement); — or it has its own ***demonstrations, ἀποδείξεις,*** ***universal experience***, ***ἐπαγωγή***, or ***principles and notions born with us, νοήσεις,*** — that is, ***the ordered understanding for constructing a true consequence.*** But these very demonstrations are ***greatly languid,*** and — insofar as they are firm and true — are to that degree a ***rivulet and a branch from the true sea and fountain of Divine Wisdom.***

But concerning the sacred writings, David says (Ps. 45): ***"Thy sceptre is a sceptre of righteousness"*** — that is, ***Thy Word, by which Thou rulest us, is certain, true, constant, always in agreement with itself, immaculate, firm and faithful, luminous, pure"*** (Ps. 19). And it has ***the most certain norms of the most certain certainty***:⁷²

1. **Divine Authority and manifestation.**
2. **Miracles proper to divine nature** — resuscitation of the dead, repression of a river and reversal of the sun, fecundity of the barren, a virgin giving birth, and others.
3. **Antiquity,** concerning which Tertullian's rule is to be observed: ***"That which is first, is truest."***
4. **Prophecies and their events.**
5. **The genus of doctrine revealing arcana** and things unknown to human reason.
6. **The series of Doctors and Restorers.**
7. **The consensus and perpetuity of doctrine,** while other human laws perish and are changed.
8. **The marvellous conservation of the Church.**
9. **The rage of the Devil persecuting the doctrine, doctors, and disciples of the Gospel.**
10. **The authority and multitude of witnesses.**
11. **The testimonies of all the Martyrs, and the constancy of the Confessors,** which show that God adds strength to the infirm against terrors and supplices, and that the Gospel is efficacious in them and is the seed of God, by which are inchoately begun eternal life, righteousness, and joy.
12. **The penalties of the impious, tyrants, and others.**
13. **The universal experience of all the pious in invocation and in the exercises of penitence.**

These are the marks of the ***Third Cause*** which ought to move us to the study of the Divine Word.

**The Fourth Cause** is the ***consideration of our fragility, of our most miserable state, and of the most perilous condition of our whole life without the Word of God.*** Our life is ***short and toilsome,*** and all that is called ***man*** in this life is rightly compared to ***vanity*** (Ps. 39, 144; Ecclesiastes 1, 9, 14, 18, and often elsewhere). Whence Bernard, describing man, says: ***"Man is nothing else than a foul seed, a sack of dung, food for worms."***

> ***"After man, a worm; after the worm, foulness and horror;***
> ***So is every man converted into this appearance."***

This misery our reason and experience sees, acknowledges, and deplores; whence also so many complaints about the fragility of human life exist among the Gentiles. Of these, three (as it were the chief and most celebrated) it is useful to recall.

**The first is of Aristotle:** *"Ti estin anthrōpos? asthenías paradígma, chrónou látrōn, tychēs átkhthos, empeirías eídōlon, phthónou kai symphorās trytánē; tò dè loipón, phlégma kai cholé."* ***"What is man? An example of weakness, a spoil of time, a plaything of fortune, an image of inconstancy, a scale of envy and calamity — the rest is phlegm and bile."***

***Nothing is weaker and more infirm than man,*** who is a ***creature of clay,*** ***dust and shadow,*** ***a bubble quickly vanishing,*** ***a fragile glass*** — as *D. Luther* said to Jonas, extending to him a beautiful glass:

> ***"To glassy Jonas, Luther himself gives glass, that as glass, glassy Jonas may know himself."***

And time — how brief and slight it is; how quickly and suddenly it passes! how each best day flees first from wretched mortals; care, sad old age, come upon [us], and man scarcely enjoys the half part of his life — since half is consumed by sleep, a great part is lived unknowingly in infancy and boyhood, and a great part is also afflicted by diseases, and at the extremity is fallen into the infirmity of age — so that it is necessary to ***"grow old,"*** since ***"we do not experience"*** [the fulness of days]. But how inconstant fortune itself is, which plays with us in various ways — so that most truly may be said:

> ***"And the certain hour hardly holds present faith. So that***
> ***When Fortune's wheel rolls all with a cautious spin,***
> ***Learn to fear God, and to trust in God."***

But of these things there will be occasion to speak in their own place. — **The Second saying is of Euripides in the *Orestes***: ***"Neither is any speech so terrible to be borne, nor is [any] lot, nor evil injected by the wrath of the Heavenly ones, which human nature by suffering does not bear."***

**The Third saying is of Pindar,** *Pythian Odes 8*: ***"Ephēmerói. Ti dé tis? Ti d' où tis? Skiās ónar ánthrōpoi."*** ***"We are men of a day. What is anyone? What is no one? The dream of a shadow are all men."*** *[And as Sophocles adds: "We see nothing else — those who live — than shadows and a light burden of nothingness."]*⁷³

*[Selnecker here inserts a fifteen-couplet elegiac lament by his colleague ***D. Wolf,*** who compresses the paradigm-classical *humanitas-fragilis* complaints into a versified summary; and then closes with the paradigm-*consolatio-in-Verbo-Dei*: without the Word of God we could rightly curse the day of our birth (like Job and Jeremiah), but with the Word we have that which is ***"more precious than life itself"*** (Ps. 63:3): ***"In the sanctuary I have seen Thee, that I might behold Thy power and Thy glory: for Thy mercy is better and more desirable than life itself."***]*⁷⁴

**The Fifth Cause** is ***the very goodness, grace, and mercy of God, His will and counsel,*** who therefore wills His doctrine of the Word to be set before us, that through it ***we may be gained, called, and led to righteousness and eternal life.*** For God receives from us this one fruit, ***that we be saved,*** and He exults only on account of the salvation of men, and to Him there is no work more grateful than this alone: ***that man be saved,*** as Clement says. For He so loves us that (if we rightly consider the matter) it seems as though His essence and life were set in us — that is, ***as if He could not be without us, nor would He wish to live without us,*** as Tauler speaks — as He declared in the sending of His Son. Wherefore let us magnify the study of the Divine Word, and let us not by our negligence, sloth, security, and perversity offend so pious, good, and propitious a God. For otherwise in our life it is most true, as is wont to be said: ***"To offend the good is a foul crime."***⁷⁵

**The Sixth Cause** ought to be the ***extreme security of men*** — which is a most certain argument of horrible penalties immediately impending. For by consideration and intuition of this we ought ***not*** to be moved to accumulate the common evil to our own destruction, but ought to ***deplore*** the blindness and malice of men — by which God has now nearly been made into an act, or a fable [a spectacle]. We ought to recognize the ***tyranny and hatred of the Devil,*** now at the end of the world raging more cruelly against the Church of God — that thus we may take up the care of our salvation, and seek and find counsel and help, consolation and patience, in the Word of God. For without the Word of God, no one in this time will go rightly, but will either associate and consort with the secure and impious world, or will rush into impatience, or into doubt about God and providence — as examples testify. Of which evil Ps. 73 complains, saying: ***"Behold, these impious ones, flourishing and rich; but I, wretched. I would think, that I might know the cause; and it was hard in my eyes, until I went into the sanctuary of God,"*** etc.

**The Seventh Cause** consists in the ***promises and threats*** which God Himself embraces: **1 Sam. 2**: ***"Whosoever shall glorify me, him will I glorify; and those who despise me shall be inglorious."*** And **John 5**: ***"He that heareth my word, and believeth on him that sent me, hath eternal life, and shall not come into judgement."*** Likewise **Luke 6**: ***"Every one that heareth my words, and doeth them, shall be likened to a wise man, who built his house upon a rock,"*** etc. **Matt. 7**: ***"Every one that heareth my words, and doeth them not, shall be likened to a foolish man, who built his house upon the sand,"*** etc.

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### Chapter 3. On the Scope of the Whole Scripture⁷⁶

**The Canon of Theology is** — that Scripture is the ***key by which we open the abscondite and hidden treasures of the Prophets*** — ***the Son of God,*** who says: ***"The Scriptures testify of me."*** Item: ***"In the volume of the book it is written of me."*** For the Law is a ***schoolmaster unto Christ,*** and the Prophets are ***κήρυκες*** (heralds) and fellow-workers; the Gospel is the ***school of Christ,*** and ***Christ is the Rabbi,*** Doctor, and Preceptor. The doctrine, however, is ***of the glory of God, and of our life, righteousness, and salvation.*** This is the ***foundation,*** concerning which in 1 Cor. 3 it is written: ***"Other foundation can no man lay than that which is laid — which is Jesus Christ."***

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### Chapter 4. On the Method of Learning the Doctrine of the Church⁷⁷

**Most rightly is it said** that he who would apply himself to the study of the Sacred Letters must have before him ***three ways*** by which he ought to enter. **The first way is directed to God the Father,** consisting of ***three degrees or steps***:

1. The ***true recognition*** of the Majesty, Essence, and Will of the Divine;
2. ***Humility and subjection,*** according to that: ***"Lord, I am not worthy that I should lift up mine eyes to heaven"*** [Luke 18:13];
3. The ***renunciation of himself and of his own powers and fortunes*** — that a man may offer, believe, hand over, commit, and consecrate the whole of himself to God, according to that: ***"Lord, we have left all, and have followed Thee"*** [Matt. 19:27].

**The second way, directed to God the Son,** must consist likewise of ***three degrees:***

1. ***Assiduous reading of the sacred writings,*** in which Christ is sought and found, as it were wrapped in swaddling clothes;
2. ***Repetition, annotation, and consignment*** of what has been read;
3. ***Meditation*** on righteousness, on the Law, on mercy, on the Gospel, on the goodness of God, and on our gratitude.

**The third way, directed to the Holy Spirit,** has likewise ***three joinings***:

1. ***Prayer;***
2. ***Experience in the cross, or temptation;***
3. ***Vocation.***

These are the ***nine steps of Jacob's Ladder,*** to which the tenth — which is ***eternal life and salvation*** — succeeds. Let these nine rules be held by students of the sacred writings, and exercised in use.⁷⁸

#### The First Rule

**Whoever wishes to know the heavenly doctrine, ought to bring a simple and pious mind** — *τῷ ἀθορύβῳ ὄμματι* — that is, ***let him not doubt of the certainty of the doctrine divinely handed down;*** ***let him not bring his own to the fountains of Israel;*** ***let him not be fascinated by preconceived opinions,*** ***let him not luxuriate in foolish desires*** — that is, ***let him not have an Epicurean, Stoic, or Academic mind,*** but such as sets ***in every way nothing before the glory of God and the salvation of souls.*** Especially in the student of the Divine Word, and in every Theologian — nay, in every Christian man — ***πνεῦμα πονηρόν and πάθος*** must be absent; and ***let each one leave his ass and servants under the mountain, as Abraham did*** — that is, ***let him not bring foolish reason and depraved affections to the divine mysteries*** or into the mountain of the Lord.⁷⁹

#### The Second Rule

**The Son of God hands down** the necessary rule for every student of the Gospel — John 7: ***"If any man will do the will of Him who sent me, he shall know of the doctrine."*** Therefore ***the doctrine is to be known from the fountains*** — that is, from the writings of the Old and New Testament.⁸⁰

#### The Third Rule

**1 Tim. 4**: ***"Be diligent in reading, teaching, and consoling."*** Wherefore the student of the Divine Word ought to have at hand a certain method of learning — so that first he may begin ***with reading of the Prophetic and Apostolic monuments*** — of which the ***terms and goal*** are to be established: namely, **doctrine and consolation** as the aim of the reading of the sacred Bible. For ***reading educates and begets certain doctrine.*** Doctrine imbues the mind with the knowledge and invocation of God and with the government of life, and kindles it to ***confidence and consolation in God*** to be conceived and retained. Consolation, the will of God being known, ***excites and confirms invocation, hope, and faith*** in souls.⁸¹

#### The Fourth Rule

**The whole body of the heavenly doctrine** is to be embraced by the mind — and then the ***native sentence of the several sayings*** is to be taken, so that (according to Paul, 2 Tim. 1:13) ***ὑποτύπωσις ὑγιαινόντων λόγων*** — the ***certain form, sum, and description of uncorrupt doctrine*** — may be before the eyes; which sees the ***whole body of Christian doctrine, and all the members rightly cohering with themselves***…

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

#### The Fifth Rule

**The learning of ***classical languages***** — the paradigm Greek eloquence (which Budaeus renders in his elegant Latin distich): ***"Eloquence alone was in our heart — as much as the Eastern and the Western tracts of the world together produced, and the illustrious glory of the Greeks at Athens: yet as soon as it had cost us much labour, we abandoned it prostrate before Christ's feet. For to the Word of the highest Godhead yields every varied and versatile speech of the human mind."***

***Augustine*** also says nobly: ***"Just as the Israelites carried the gold and silver vessels out of Egypt with them, and transferred them to the true use and worship of God — so Christians ought to know the writings, arts, and philosophy of the Gentiles, and to transfer them to the glory of God."***⁸²

#### The Sixth Rule

Let there be a ***judgement of interpreters,*** and let those be held who are the illustration of the Prophetic and Apostolic books, and open acknowledgement of the true doctrine of the whole Church. But approved and authentic Doctors of the Church are to be read — whose faith and constancy have profited the Church — and one must not easily applaud whatever writings, but hold the rule of 1 Thess. 5:21: ***"Prove all things: hold fast that which is good."*** And Jer. 6:16: ***"Ask for the old paths, which is the good way, and walk therein."*** And ***Augustine*** on the writings of all Doctors of the Church says: ***"If anything is confirmed by the manifest authority of the divine writings which are called Canonical, without any doubt it is to be believed; but of other testimonies or witnesses, by which anything is persuaded to be believed, it may be lawful for you either to believe or not to believe, according as you weigh the merit either to have or not to have of belief."*** And elsewhere on his own writings he says: ***"I do not wish you to be enslaved to my writings as if to Canonical [Scripture]; but if perchance you find in them what you did not believe, believe unhesitatingly; but in these, what you have not certainly, unless you have understood it certainly, do not firmly believe."***⁸³

#### The Seventh Rule

The ***voice of the ministry divinely instituted in the temples and schools*** is to be heard, and judgements are to be conferred with other pious and learned [men]. As it is written in Judges 14 [of Samson's riddle]: ***"If ye had not ploughed with my heifer, ye had not found out my riddle"*** — that is, ***unless you had heard the doctrine of the Church, you would err by a whole heaven from the true sense of the Gospel.*** And experience testifies that ***fleers of the light*** commonly bring forth ***new kinds of doctrines.***⁸⁴

#### The Eighth Rule

The ***distinction of Law and Gospel*** is to be diligently observed and held — as Paul warns (2 Tim. 2:15): ***"Rightly dividing the word,"*** ὀρθοτομοῦντα, wherewith he demands that care be applied in distinguishing the diverse members of doctrine — Law, Gospel, Philosophy, Political governance, and many others. For with ***conspicuous limits, as it were in a judgement of governed boundaries,*** must Political wisdom and the doctrine proper to the Church be distinguished; and the ***foul and pernicious confusions of these kinds*** are to be dreaded.⁸⁵

#### The Ninth Rule

Let there be added at last the ***exercise of penitence and invocation.*** For in true penitence, faith, invocation, hope, and patience (or in bearing the cross), the whole doctrine of the Gospel is made ***more illustrious,*** as it is written in the Psalm [119:71]: ***"It is good for me, O Lord, that Thou hast humbled me, that I might learn Thy justifications."***

From these rules students of piety and doctrine can easily gather what their office ought to be. But I add also this ***admonition***: he who wishes to be exercised with any fruit in the school of Christ ought to bring with him ***four immoveable and infallible principles.***

**The First**: ***That there is only one eternal, true, omnipotent, and merciful God*** — which principle must be fixed against the multitude of Gods of the Gentiles, and against the Manichees.

**The Second**: Not only that there is a God, and that the care of men before all other bodily creatures is to Him — but also that this same God has ***manifested His essence and will to us in His Word,*** comprehended in the writings of Moses, the Prophets, and the Apostles; and that the supreme good and last end of man, to which man has been established, is the ***knowledge and celebration of the true God*** according to His own manifestation.

**The Third**: That we ought to feel, speak, believe, live, die, be raised, and be saved ***according to the norm and prescription of the divine Word*** — that true knowledge, righteousness, life, and salvation are to be sought from no other source than the Word of God — since God does not wish to be known or worshipped for eternity otherwise than as He has manifested Himself.

**The Fourth**: That whatever is besides this Word ***cannot be judged necessary to salvation***; and whatever fights against this Word ***ought to be anathema.***

These are the ***principles of Theology*** — which whoever shall faithfully have held, may be safe against both the idolatry of Gentiles and Turks, and against human traditions and inventions.⁸⁶

### Chapter 5. On the Method to be Observed in the Explication of the Heavenly Doctrine⁸⁷

*[Selnecker here treats the paradigm ***Wittenberg-Melanchthonian pedagogical framework*** for the sequential explication of the heads of Christian doctrine — following the paradigm ***Hermogenes*** rhetorical-progression schema and the paradigm ***Rudolphi-medical progression*** analogy (from Constitution of the body → Causes and differences → Signs → Cures). The paradigm-classical model Selnecker recommends is **Melanchthon's *Loci Communes*** (mature 1543/1555 editions) as the paradigm ***loci-communes-form*** for organizing dogmatic instruction. He also acknowledges — with paradigm ***moderate-medieval-appreciation*** — the ***Peter Lombard*** paradigm-***Sententiae*** (Paris, c. 1150 — issued 1172 in Selnecker's paradigm-dating) which trained two centuries of medieval theologians, and — with paradigm-critical distance — the paradigm ***two-hundred*** subsequent scholastic ***commentators-on-Lombard.*** The paradigm ***Panoplia dogmatica*** (a paradigm-Chrysostom-Basil-fathers armour-of-doctrine anthology reported extant in the ***Vatican Library*** at Rome in Selnecker's day) is offered as the paradigm-*positive-model* for such systematic-*loci* work. — Selnecker's overall recommendation: *loci-communes-format* with paradigm ***rudibus-initio-summam-recte-ordine-perspicue-proponi*** — *"to set forth to the rude, at the beginning, the sum rightly, in order, and perspicuously."*]*⁸⁸

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### Chapter 6. On the Manifestation of God — De Patefactione Dei⁸⁹

**In the Old Testament** God ***manifested Himself*** to the human race in ***many ways***: by ***express Word,*** ***visions,*** ***dreams,*** ***miracles,*** ***sacrifices,*** and ***types.*** We rightly say that God has manifested Himself to us: (1) by the ***Word;*** (2) by the ***mission of His Son;*** (3) by ***spiritual and corporeal benefits*** (or ***miracles***); and (4) by the ***Sacraments.***

If, however, we wish to consider and conjoin not only the Church but also the remaining crowd of the human race, we rightly say there are ***three modes*** by which God has manifested Himself to us:

**The First** consists in the ***inspection and consideration of this whole workshop, palace, and theatre*** which we call ***the world.***

**The Second** is the ***secret spiration or inspiration*** — special and secret — by which formerly the ***Patriarchs and Prophets*** were divinely taught many things: in ***visions, dreams, images,*** and ***miracles***, θεοφανείαις — whence the Prophets are named ***Choim*** — that is, ***seers.***

**The Third** is the ***living, infallible, and certain voice*** directed to all and to each — the universal promise, the doctrine of Law and Gospel, ***enduring even to the end of the world.*** For since it is ***impossible without God to learn God,*** God through His Word ***teaches men to know God*** — and thus God is the author of the doctrine of God.⁹⁰

**Otherwise and more plainly** these things are recalled thus: God has manifested Himself to the human race *first* in ***creation,*** manifesting His ***goodness, wisdom, and power***, and communicating His ***joy, life, and righteousness*** to angels and men — ***the very first bearing witness of Himself.*** Then, after the fall of the human race, He manifested Himself by the ***promise of the Gospel,*** the ***promulgation of the Law,*** and the ***mission of the only-begotten Son into our flesh.*** Then afterward by the ***visible mission of the Holy Spirit*** and in other manners.

Let there be, for the younger [students], this description of the divine manifestation, of whatever kind:

***Manifestation of God is an external action of God, by which God — from His hidden seat and light unknown to all creatures — by immense goodness and mercy proceeded forth, and by divine deliberation and will fabricated all things from nothing, and to the rational creatures communicated His wisdom, righteousness, life, and joy — and familiarly addressed men, and with His own voice handed down the Law and the Gospel, and added testimonies in no wise deceiving of His doctrine (the raising of the dead, and others), that He might testify Himself to be affected with paternal care of us; and that the Church, gathered by God out of the human race, might have a certain norm by which she might direct all her thoughts of God and her invocation — that thus the glory of God might be illustrated, and the salvation of men be certain; for whose cause God Himself, as a Bridegroom, came forth from His chamber, and constituted for Himself an eternal Church, by which He might be known and celebrated.***⁹¹

Four things are here to be considered: first, that the doctrine of manifestation is ***altogether necessary;*** second, that this manifestation is an ***immense benefit of God;*** third, ***what is the end of manifestation;*** and fourth, ***how this doctrine of the manifestation of God is to be used.***

*[Selnecker's paradigm four-fold treatment continues: (1) The necessity of manifestation — since without divine self-disclosure, no creature could know God's essence or will; (2) The immensity of the benefit — that the Creator condescends to speak with creatures; (3) The end — the knowledge, love, worship, and eternal enjoyment of God; (4) The use — the paradigm *lectio-meditatio-oratio-gratitudo-obedientia* framework of pious Bible-reading. This chapter closes Locus I with the paradigm ***transition into Locus II De Deo*** — since the very ***manifestation of God*** presupposes an inquiry into ***the manifested God Himself.***]*⁹²

# Locus II

## De Deo — On God⁹³

### Chapter 1. That There is a God — *Quod sit Deus*

**All sound-minded Gentiles have said** that the ***beginnings of all things must be taken from the immortal God.*** And that there is a God is ***innate and as it were engraved*** upon all. For the ***commodities we use,*** the ***light we enjoy,*** the ***spirit we breathe*** — we see given and imparted to us by God. And of men, there is no nation so untamed, so iron, so ferocious and immane, which — though it may not know ***what sort of God should be had*** — yet does not know that ***a God must be had.***

Although occasionally there have been those who said that ***there are no Gods,*** or that ***they know least of all whether there be any*** — as ***Protagoras,*** who denied that he could know whether there was any divinity or not (for which cause he was cast out of the confines of the Athenians); and ***Cicero*** relates various things of ***Diagoras*** which are not to be borne, whence he was called ***atheist*** — yet ***the more sound-minded Philosophers*** and all Gentiles have always detested the ***delirium*** which feigned that there is no God, and have with one accord established: ***"There is a God, who rules, who governs, who conserves the course of the stars, the changes of times, the vicissitudes of things, orders — contemplating lands and seas, protecting the commodities and lives of men,"*** to use Cicero's words.⁹⁴

Certainly in the Church not only must ***the true God*** be established, but even this very question, ***"Is there a God?"*** — is not freed of suspicion of some blasphemy — until, for the instruction of ruder souls (who are yet to be fed with mother's milk), it is set forth for the confirmation of the true sentence concerning God. For pious minds may more easily deny ***heaven, earth, and all creatures*** (which nevertheless is by no means to be believed possible) than to doubt that there is a God, the Creator and Conserver of all things.

### Chapter 2. Whence it is Certainly Established that there is a God

**There is a twofold knowledge or acknowledgement of God:** ***Universal*** and ***Particular.*** The former pertains to all men. The latter is proper to the ***sons of God,*** or to the ***Church.*** Although, even if no demonstrations were to appear, we should still by ***faith*** believe that God is (as the Epistle to the Hebrews chapter 11 says), yet it profits to have at hand ***natural reasons or [reasons] impressed by nature,*** which testify that there is a God.⁹⁵

What pertains to the ***universal knowledge of God*** — of which Paul preaches to the Romans 1 and 2 — let these illustrious and infallible arguments be held:

**The First** — from the ***marvellous magnitude, disposition, and ornament of the whole world and of its parts*** — whether heaven and the innumerable stars be considered, or earth, living things, and their kinds, or whatever other things, wheresoever they may be. On this argument let Job 9 be read, and Ps. 19: ***"The heavens declare the glory of God, and the firmament announces the works of His hands."*** And Ps. 104, in which David gives thanks to God for the whole re-creation — from which is known ***both that He is, and what sort He is*** — namely wise, good, and just. And hither refer these verses:

> ***"He who denies that God is — let him presently look upon the stars;***
> ***And who looks upon the stars — swears that God is."***
> ***"Either the sole of my foot proves God from the herbs;***
> ***And a single herb declares God present."***
> ***"The whole series of works recounts its Author,***
> ***who makes and by wide vigour cherishes all."***
> ***"The divine power shines from the very fields;***
> ***And light is the sod that proves that God is."***

**The Second argument** is taken from the ***perpetual similitude and conservation of species.*** From man, man is born; from ox, ox. And not only the universal conservation and propagation of species in the whole nature of things shows that God is, but also the ***mode of propagation and generation of species*** is most worthy of admiration, and shows how great is the wisdom of the Creator — that from a root or a granule cast into the earth and putrefied, plants of the same species are born.

**The Third** — from the ***vicissitude of times***, in which the celestial orbs and the fixed stars, by certain laws, revolve around, and render to us day and night, summer and winter, sowing and reaping, serenity and rains. To this third argument let us join also the ***ordinary revolutions of the celestial luminaries,*** which by motion, light, and influx serve the inferior things.

**The Fourth** — from the ***illustrious notions and gifts in men,*** which themselves also testify and confess ***that God is*** — especially when the ***distinction of honest and base*** is considered; which certainly is ***not casual,*** but is a ***certain and eternal rule,*** discerning good from evils; which rule has been received from another…⁹⁶

…[which rule has been received] from some certain intelligent cause. Hither is also to be referred the ***excellence of the whole man*** — who is the ***paradigm-image both of the whole world and of God Himself*** — showing both that God is, and in great part what sort God is. Whether the structure of the human body, its form, proportion, affection, and use be considered; or the powers of the soul, the offices of mind, reason, and will; or discipline, industry, and the care of business; or heroic motions and successes in things to be done; or premonitions of future things and of the greatest events; or the terrors, fears, judgements, and torments of conscience accompanying atrocious crimes of the soul, be pondered. For all these things which are placed in man's nature ***manifestly show that God is, and what sort He is*** — namely wise, just, good, ***vindicator of justice, judge, punisher of crimes.***

**The Fifth argument** is drawn from the ***order and connection of causes,*** and the ***perpetual motion of things,*** which necessarily must be moved by some other — according to that: ***"Everything that is moved, is moved by another."*** Item, ***"There is no progress into infinity, but it is necessary to stop at some first mover,"*** which is the beginning and cause of the motion of all others.

**The Sixth** — from ***signs,*** partly ***natural and ordinary,*** partly ***extraordinary,*** concerning which the Son of God says (Luke 21): ***"There shall be signs in the Sun, Moon, and stars"*** — as are frequent eclipses of Sun and Moon, remarkable conjunctions or oppositions of planets, sudden tempests, meteors, earthquakes, pestilences, various diseases, wars, terrors, and many similar things — of which the Gentiles said: ***"When God is angry, the sons of heroes flee."***

**The Seventh** — from ***rewards of the good and punishments of the wicked,*** which regularly accompany the deeds of men, and show that God is, who repays to each one according to his works — as [Homer] says: ***"θεοβλαβὴς πάντα ὁρῶν Θεός."*** ***"God, the vindicator, sees all things."***

**The Eighth** — from ***political society*** and the ***dispensation and administration of human affairs*** — from constituted empires, polities, and commonwealths; from established salutary rules; from the distinct orders of men; from preserved obedience and discipline; from certain periods of kingdoms — and many others — of which also Plato says: ***"God sets governors over cities."***

**The Ninth** is taken from ***final causes.*** All things are established for certain uses. This distribution of ends did not exist by chance, nor does it remain by chance, but is to be referred to the saying of Genesis 1: ***"All things which God had made were very good."***

These arguments it profits often to consider, since they both confirm the good, and at the same time show the ***infirmity of the human mind.*** And the ***use of this universal knowledge of God*** is that all men may be ***inexcusable*** who — through wisdom, that is, through the consideration of universal nature (which everywhere is inclined and prompt to confess God) — do not acknowledge the wise God (1 Cor. 1) — even though He is ever near at hand, so that men themselves are His kind (Acts 17), and are in Him, live, and move in Him — and can from the whole machine of the world know His ***Power,*** from the disposition of things His ***Wisdom,*** from conservation His ***Goodness,*** from administration His ***Justice,*** from patience and long-suffering His ***Mercy.***

But since so great is the ***caligo*** (darkness) of the human mind, that the ***book of Antony the Eremite*** does not suffice for the true knowledge of God — that is, the contemplation of the world does not educate the souls of men concerning the true God, but only shows to us ***a certain shadow of the wisdom and goodness of God*** — therefore there is need of a certain ***peculiar knowledge of God,*** which rests on a ***two-fold foundation:*** the ***Word*** and the ***Testimony of God Himself,*** manifesting His essence and will to the human race in Word and Testimony, and wishing that He be so known, apprehended, invoked, and celebrated, as He has manifested Himself through Word and Testimony. And the ***Word*** is named the ***doctrine of Law and Gospel,*** thence from the beginning divinely handed down, and comprised in the writings of the Prophets and Apostles. The ***Testimony*** is named the confirmation of the doctrine itself, as well as the confidence conceived from the doctrine. For there is a two-fold testimony: one, which is added to the Word to confirm it, that from men it may be held as true, firm, and ratified; another, which is added as a ***seal of faith*** and a ***sacrament,*** by which the confidence conceived from the Word is to be confirmed. And this ***knowledge of God*** which rests on Word and Testimony alone is ***salutary,*** as it is written in Wisdom 15: ***"To know God is consummate righteousness, and to know His righteousness and virtue is the root of immortality."*** And John 17: ***"This is eternal life, that they may know Thee the only true God, and Him whom Thou hast sent, Jesus Christ."*** And Jer. 9: ***"Let him that glorieth glory in this — to know and to acknowledge me: for I am Jehovah, and I do mercy, and judgement, and justice in the earth"*** — that is, ***I give good things, I put to death and quicken, or justify. For these please me, saith the Lord.***⁹⁷

#### What is the Distinction between the Universal Notion of God (Philosophical, or common to all men) and the Peculiar (or Christian)?

The greatest part of men have this opinion — ***that all religions are equal,*** and that every one is saved by his own faith, whatever it be, provided only that it proceed from a soul studious of religion. And many feign that ***Aristides is no less just, and heir of eternal life, than Paul.***

That we may flee these hideous errors, let us know from the Word of God — in which the essence and will of God have been manifested to us — that ***there is a threefold distinction*** between the notion of God which is common to all men (even those outside the Church) and the ***other, proper to the Church,*** which is to be received.

**First**: although sound-minded Gentiles affirm that God is, yet ***of the three Persons — Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — they know nothing.***

**Second**: although Gentiles somehow knew the will of God manifested in the Law, yet they did not know the ***whole Law*** — especially in what pertains to the worships of the First Table. As Paul himself says (Rom. 7): ***"I would not have known that concupiscence was sin, unless the Law had said, 'Thou shalt not covet.'"***

**Third**: ***all Gentiles are ignorant of the will of God manifested in the Gospel*** concerning the remission of sins and eternal life on account of the Son the Mediator. For ***"no one knoweth the Father save the Son, and he to whom the Son shall have willed to reveal Him"*** (Matt. 11). ***"No one hath ever seen God: the Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, He hath declared Him"*** (John 1). And John 14: Christ says: ***"No one cometh to the Father, save through me. If ye had known me, ye would also have known my Father: he that seeth me, seeth the Father."*** And 1 Cor. 2 it is written: ***"To us hath God revealed [these things] through His Spirit."***

#### What is God?

**The Gentiles, being ignorant of the essence and will of God manifested in the Word and Testimony, have invented infinite opinions of God** — and to the very creatures, the heavenly luminaries, the elements, men, animals, and even statues destitute of all sense, they have attributed the appellation of ***Gods*** and ***Numina*** — whence idols nearly innumerable, idolatrous worships, and horrible abominations have derived their origin. And ***"thirty thousand idols have been adored among the nations,"*** Augustine attests. ***"Three hundred Joves*** [have been] ***among the nations,"*** Terentius Varro writes. And concerning this shameful and various religion of the nations, the book of Wisdom, chapter 13, 14, and 15, most vehemently complains, as has been said in its place.

**Let us therefore give thanks to God for the manifestation,** and let us seek a ***definition or description of God*** in the Word divinely handed down and commended to us — of which Irenaeus, book 4, chapter 10, nobly says thus: ***"Since it was impossible without God to learn God, He teaches men through His Word to know God."*** And Hilary says: ***"Let us understand this alone to be well believed concerning God, to the belief of which He Himself, both author and witness, has borne testimony."***

***What God is simply,*** and ***what is the nature and substance of God*** — let us know that ***no one can constitute, imagine, comprehend, and speak*** by any essential definition, or any Dialectic reason, or by the edge of human genius. For since ***"eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man, what things God hath prepared for those who love Him"*** — how much less shall the ***dullness of the human mind*** attain to God Himself?

Whence many were wont to say: **"It can more easily be defined what God is not, than what He is."** And the response of Simonides, given to Hiero of Sicily, is known — who, after several days had elapsed, and time to deliberate and meditate had often been granted, was unable to determine anything certain concerning what God should be; the longer this matter was considered, the more obscure it seemed to the ponderers. ***But many object:***

- ***"No one can believe what he knows not.***
- ***We do not know what God is.***
- ***Therefore our faith concerning God is either nothing, or is uncertain and maimed."***

**I answer to the major:** No one can believe what he knows not — namely, that which ***wholly and altogether he knows not.*** **To the minor:** ***"What God is, we know not"*** — namely, in what pertains to the ***nature or substance of God*** simply considered. But we ***do not not-know*** that ***one eternal essence of God is in three distinct Persons,*** as God has manifested Himself. And just as one hearing at night in the temple a shepherd preaching, ***knows that he is there, and that he is a good and pious shepherd, yet does not see his face plainly*** — so we know God's Trinity from His own manifestation.¹⁰¹ᵃ

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### Chapter 3. The Definition of God — De Definitione Dei⁹⁸

*(pp. 66–68 of the imprint.)*

**God is one divine Essence, spiritual, intelligent, eternal, truthful, good, just, chaste, merciful, most free, of immense power and wisdom** — *Father eternal, who from eternity begat the Son His image; and Son, image of the Father co-eternal, begotten by the Father's saying or thought; and Holy Spirit, proceeding from the Father and the Son* — as the Divinity has been manifested by certain Word, testimony, and miracle: *who together with the Son and the Holy Spirit has founded, sustains, and preserves heaven and earth and all creatures; and in the human race established to His own image, chooses for Himself the Church — that from her He may be known, celebrated, and eternally worshipped*, all other worships condemned and rejected which outside the Word of God feign other Gods.

This definition (if it may be so called) is comprised in ***five members.*** **The first part** recites the ***proprieties or attributes of God*** — which in God are not accidents but the very essence of God signified by various appellations — showing ***what God is absolutely.*** As Augustine says nobly and piously: ***"God is great without quantity, and good without quality."***⁹⁹

The first member of the definition is: **God is one divine Essence, spiritual** (John 4: *πνεῦμα* — that is, without corporeal mass); **Intelligent** (from whom every intellect has arisen, Ps. 94: *"He who planted the ear,"* etc.); **Eternal** — not arisen from elsewhere, but having being from Himself from eternity (Isa. 44: *"I am the first and the last";* John 5: *"As the Father hath life in Himself, so hath He given to the Son to have life in Himself"*); **Truthful** — ***"God is ἀληθής,"*** while ***lying is from the Devil*** (John 8); **Good** (Titus 3: *χρηστότης* — beneficence, liberality, humanity); **Just** (Ps. 119: *"Thou art just, O Lord, and just is Thy judgement";* Dan. 9); **Chaste** (Heb. 12: *"Without sanctification no man shall see God";* Heb. 13; 1 Cor. 1; Matt. 5: *"Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God"*); **Merciful** (Ps. 103; Rom. 5: *"Grace superabounds over sin"*); **Most free** (against the Stoics, Prov. 16: *"The Lord hath wrought all things for Himself"*); **Of immense Power** (Ps. 135: *"All things that He hath willed, He hath done";* Luke 1: *"With God no word is impossible"*); and **of immense Wisdom** (Ps. 147: *"His wisdom hath no number"*).

Hitherto philosophical definitions somehow agree, though they are very ***bald and thin,*** and cannot rightly explain any attribute of God. There follows now the second part of the definition, which shows ***what God is relatively.***

**The second part** describes the ***internal relations*** of the three Persons: **Father eternal,** who from eternity begat the Son His image; **Son,** image of the Father co-eternal, begotten by the Father's saying or thought; **Holy Spirit,** proceeding from the Father and the Son — as the Divinity has been manifested by certain Word, testimony, and miracle.¹⁰⁰

**The third part** describes God from the ***general effect*** — that is, from the creation, sustenance, and conservation of all things — namely that the Father eternal, with the Son and Holy Spirit, has founded, sustains, and preserves heaven and earth and all creatures.

**The fourth part** describes God from the ***spiritual effect*** — that is, from the gathering and conservation of the Church in this life — that in the human race established to His image, He chooses for Himself the Church.

**The fifth part** shows the ***end of divine manifestation*** — that from the Church God may be known, celebrated, and eternally worshipped, all other worships condemned and rejected which outside the Word of God feign other Gods.

This description is ***whole,*** drawn from the Word of God, so much as in this life for the certification of our salvation is necessary for us to know. And it is necessary ***to distinguish absolute divinity considered in one Essence*** from the ***eternal and peculiar substance and relation of the Persons to one another.*** For divinity is considered ***otherwise ἀπροσώπως*** (impersonally), ***otherwise προσώπως*** (personally), as appears from what has been said.¹⁰¹

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### Chapter 4. On the Distinction of the Persons — De Distinctione Personarum¹⁰²

*(pp. 81–85 of the imprint.)*

**In the personal distinction, first it is to be seen how the whole Divinity is separated from creatures** — namely, by ***five properties*** which pertain to God alone, and to no creature:

1. To be ***eternal and omnipotent.***
2. To be ***three distinct Persons in one individual and indistinct essence,*** not multiplied.
3. To be the ***Creator of all things.***
4. To be ***in the world, and above the world.***
5. To be ***in every place, and in no place,*** and *αὐτοπρόσωπος.*

**Then it is to be seen how the Persons are distinguished among themselves** — namely, ***in a two-fold way,*** by ***interior*** and ***exterior*** properties. The interior [properties] are: ***to beget, to be born, to proceed.*** For only the Father begets the Son; only the Son is born of the Father; and only the Holy Spirit proceeds from both.

The exterior properties are: ***to send*** and ***to be sent.*** Only the Father sends; the Son and the Holy Spirit are sent — but ***in different manner.*** The Son, sent, assumes human nature. The Holy Spirit, sent into the hearts of men, kindles in them ***a new light.*** And although the ***whole Trinity*** can be said ***to send*** — so that the Son sends Himself, or is sent by Himself, and the Holy Spirit also sends Himself, and gives and communicates Himself to us (as Augustine in many places disputes) — yet by reason of ***the divine manifestation and of the principle from which the mission is,*** ***only the Father*** is said to send, and not to be sent; but the Son and the Holy Spirit are sent — nor yet are they excluded from ***active mission.*** For the Son and the Holy Spirit send themselves and one another, ***by reason of the invisible Divinity;*** but they are sent, ***by reason of the manifestation,*** in which the Divinity reveals and communicates itself to us.¹⁰²ᵃ

Hither pertain the ***two Rules of Augustine,*** of which above also it has been spoken:

**[Rule 1.] The works of the Trinity ***ad intra*** are divided** — that is, the works which God does ***before and outside every creature*** are distinct, and distinguish the Persons of the Divinity. The work of God the Father ***ad intra,*** or His proper internal action, is that ***the Father is made by no one, nor created, nor begotten,*** but Himself from eternity, existing without beginning and from Himself, ***begets the Son as image of Himself.*** This work — namely, to beget the Son and to communicate to Him His own essence — is distinct from the work of the Son and Holy Spirit.

The ***proper*** [work] ***of the Son*** is: that ***He is by no one created, nor made, but begotten of the eternal Father by an eternal generation,*** with essence communicated. This work — namely, to be begotten of the Father — is distinct from the work of the Father and of the Holy Spirit.

The ***proper*** [work] ***of the Holy Spirit*** is: that ***He is not created, nor made, nor begotten, but proceeds from the Father and the Son,*** with the essence of the Father and Son communicated. By these ***internal actions,*** therefore, the Persons of the Deity are distinguished ***from eternity unto eternity;*** and by this manner ***in the essence unity, and in the Persons propriety*** can be retained — so that neither the ***one essence*** is drawn asunder into many, nor the ***confusion of Persons*** is made.

Whence ***Nazianzen*** says: ***πάντα τοῦ πατρὸς, καὶ τοῦ Υἱοῦ ἴδια, πλὴν τῆς ἀγεννησίας· πάντα τὰ τοῦ Υἱοῦ, καὶ τοῦ Πνεύματος, πλὴν τῆς γεννήσεως. Οὐκ ἀφαιροῦνται τὴν οὐσίαν, ἀλλὰ περὶ τὴν οὐσίαν διαφέρουσι.*** — *"All that the Father is, is proper also to the Son, save that the Father is unbegotten; all that the Son is, is proper also to the Spirit, save begetting. These do not separate the essence, but are distinguished round about the essence. Are you eager for contradictions? Restrain thy tongue, and honour the day of the Spirit. Concerning other tongues let there be speech, but reverence these."***¹⁰²ᵇ

**[Rule 2.] The works of the Trinity ***ad extra*** are undivided** — that is, the works which God does ***outside His essence*** are common to the three Persons, yet the propriety of each Person is preserved. Thus ***creation*** is the work of God alone externally — no partaker of the divine essence [being permitted] — and is equally a work made by the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. ***Salvation*** is the work of the whole Trinity through the Son sent, and ordained for the redemption of the human race, and accomplished. ***Sanctification*** is the work of the whole Trinity, which is effected through the Holy Spirit, sent into the hearts of believers.

Hence it is said: ***"The Son acts through Himself, but not from Himself"*** — that is, the Son executes and accomplishes the will and counsel of the whole Trinity in redeeming the human race, and does this both in His own Person, in His will and free obedience, and also by divine power and efficacy — sent by the Father, and ordained and constituted to this end, that He may be Redeemer and Saviour of the human race — as He Himself says (John 8): ***"I have not come from myself, but the Father hath sent me."*** Item: ***"I am not alone, but I, and the Father who sent me."***

On this singular mystery, Augustine against the Jews on the Incarnation of the Lord speaks thus: ***"Look at the cithara of the player, that he may render musical melody with sweet sounds. Three things are seen to be present equally: art, hand, string — and yet one sound is heard. Art dictates, the hand touches, the string resounds. Three things equally operate, yet the string alone resounds; nor does the art nor the hand yield sound, but they operate together with the string. So neither the Father nor the Holy Spirit assumed flesh, and yet with the Son they equally operate. The sound the string alone gives forth; the flesh Christ alone received; the operation stands in the three. But as the giving of the sound pertains to the string alone, so pertains to Christ alone the reception of the human flesh."***¹⁰²ᶜ

From these things which have been said, such ***description of the Persons*** can be taken and constituted:

**The eternal Father is the First Person of the Divinity,** having essence and life from Himself, and communicating His eternal essence to the Son, His essential image begotten of Himself, and to the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father and the Son — who together with the Son and the Holy Spirit created and conserves all things visible and invisible, and sends the Son and the Holy Spirit to His Church, and through their ministry is efficacious unto salvation for every believer.

**The Son of God is the Second Person of the Divinity,** ***not created, nor made from nothing, but begotten from eternity from the Father,*** substantial and integral image of the Father — ***ὁμοούσιος τῷ Πατρί, ἐκ τῆς οὐσίας τοῦ Πατρός, θεὸς ἀληθινὸς ἐκ θεοῦ ἀληθινοῦ*** *(consubstantial with the Father, from the essence of the Father, very God of very God)* — through whom the Father created all things, and immediately manifested the Gospel: who ***Son*** in a certain time was born of the Virgin Mary and assumed human nature, and was made true man, that He might be for us [Redeemer].¹⁰²ᵈ

### Chapter 5. On God the Father — De Deo Patre¹⁰⁴

**That God the Father is a distinct Person from the Son and Holy Spirit** — the testimonies of the Old and New Testament attest. In the Old Testament it is often said that ***God sent His Word, and His Spirit,*** and that He effected something ***by His Word or Spirit*** — as it is written in Gen. 1: ***"The Spirit of God moved upon the waters."*** And Ps. 33: ***"By the Word of the Lord,"*** etc. Ps. 107: ***"He sent His Word, and healed them."*** In the New Testament, however, Christ Himself and the Apostles ***distinctly name God the Father separately,*** and evidently distinguish [Him] from the Son and Holy Spirit — as at Christ's Baptism it manifestly appears, and the same signifies the voice of the Son of God commanding the nations to be baptised ***in the name*** *(singular, showing unity in Divinity)* ***of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit*** *(three distinct appellations describing the Trinity)*.

Testimonies: **John 10:30**: ***"I and the Father are one."*** **John 14:16**: ***"I will pray the Father, and He shall give you another Comforter."*** **John 15:26**: ***"I will send unto you the Comforter from the Father, the Spirit of truth."*** **John 17:5**: ***"Glorify Thou me, Father, with Thyself, with the glory which I had with Thee before this world was."*** **Rom. 1**: ***"Paul, separated unto the Gospel of God, which He had promised concerning His Son, who was declared to be the Son of God by the sanctifying Spirit."*** **2 Cor. 13:14**: ***"The grace of the Lord Jesus Christ, and the love of God, and the communion of the Holy Spirit be with you."*** **Gal. 4:6**: ***"God sent forth the Spirit of His Son into our hearts, crying, Abba, Father."*** **Eph. 1**: ***"The God of our Lord Jesus Christ, the Father of glory, give you the Spirit of wisdom and of revelation."*** **Eph. 2**: ***"Through the Son we have access in one Spirit unto the Father."*** **Eph. 3**: ***"I bow my knees to the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, that He would grant you riches of His glory, that ye may be strengthened with might through His Spirit."***

From these testimonies it is manifest that God the Father is the ***First Person in the Trinity, hypostatically distinct*** from the Son and Holy Spirit. But the Father is distinguished from the Son and Holy Spirit ***not by reason of eternity, equality, essence, or dignity***; nor is He called the First Person because He is ***prior*** to the Son or Holy Spirit. Rather, He is called the First Person because our mind, by a certain natural instinct, best apprehends this ***order,*** and rightly places the Father in the first place, the Son in the second, and the Spirit proceeding from both in the third — especially since the Father has being ***as it were the cause, principle, and origin of universal things.***

But the Father differs from the Son in that He is and is called ***the Person begetting the Son, with essence communicated,*** according to that of Ps. 2: ***"I have this day begotten Thee."*** Of this generation something will have to be said in the place on the Son of God.¹⁰⁵

*(Selnecker adds that the term ***"Father"*** is used by us in a paradigm-***double-respect***: (1) with respect to the ***only-begotten Son*** — in this respect only the First Person of the Trinity is and is called Father; (2) with respect to ***men*** — in this respect not any one Person, but the ***whole Trinity*** obtains the name of Father — by reason of creation, government, adoption, donation, and conservation. Therefore in our Lord's Prayer we address God: ***"Our Father, who art in heaven"*** — where ***"Father"*** is the name of essence, or of the whole Divinity, bearing toward us a paternal disposition, as it is written: ***"As a father pitieth his children, so the Lord pitieth them that fear Him."***)*

### Chapter 6. On God the Son — De Deo Filio¹⁰⁶

*(pp. 89–108 of the imprint.)*

**That the Son is a Person distinct from the Father and the Holy Spirit** is attested by: (1) the ***manifestation of the three Persons*** in Christ's Baptism, and on Mount Tabor; (2) the ***appellations***, which are ***Filius, Genitivus, Unigenitus***; (3) manifestly the ***assumption of human nature from the Virgin Mary*** shows this. For neither the Father, nor the Holy Spirit assumed human nature, but ***only the Son.***

But what is said — that ***"the works ad extra are common"*** — namely, that the assumption of human nature and the work of the salvation of men is a work ad extra, therefore of the Father and Holy Spirit as of the Son — is to be answered thus: the rule of Augustine is most true, that ***the works ad extra are undivided*** — provided this be added: ***"with the propriety of each Person preserved,"*** by which the Persons are distinguished among themselves.

**The Father does not assume human nature,** because He is the Father, and Person begetting. **The Son assumes human nature,** because the propriety of the Son is to be born; and since He is the eternal Son of God, He is made also the Son of Man, and the eternal image of the Father joins to Himself human nature established to the image of God — as it is written (Prov. 8): ***"My delights were to be with the sons of men,"*** says the Wisdom of the Father — that is, the Son of God, through whom and on account of whom (***ἀγαπητός, monogenēs***) all things have been established, as Paul says (Col. 1). Whence Augustine says (whose words are pleasant here to repeat, as above cited): ***"Look at the cithara of the player, that he may render musical melody with sweet sounds. Three things are seen to be present equally: art, hand, string — and yet one sound is heard. Art dictates, the hand touches, the string resounds. Three things equally operate, yet the string alone resounds; nor does the art nor the hand yield sound, but they operate together with the string. So neither the Father nor the Holy Spirit received flesh, and yet with the Son they equally operate. The sound the string alone gives forth; the flesh Christ alone received; the operation stands in the three. But as the giving of the sound pertains to the string alone, so pertains to Christ alone the reception of the human flesh."*** The same is also said in these words: ***"The Son acts through Himself, but not from Himself."***¹⁰⁶ᵃ

#### The Five Chief Appellations of the Son of God

Now what pertains to the appellations of the Son of God — by which His properties are indicated — let us know that ***there are five chief appellations,*** which He obtains as ὁμοούσιος (consubstantial) with the Father and Holy Spirit — which Holy Scripture attributes to Him — and which He alone obtains ***from eternity into eternity,*** even had no creature ever been — and by which He is also distinguished from the Father and Holy Spirit, and by which His works ***ad intra*** are expressed.

**1. FILIUS (Son).** Ps. 2: ***"Thou art my Son: this day have I begotten thee."*** Ps. 72: ***"Before the Sun*** *(Jinnon,* that is, ***filiatum est nomen eius* — 'his name is *sonified*'*)*.*** *For "Nin" signifies a little son.* Prov. 30: ***"What is the name of His Son?"*** Dan. 3; Matt. 3. For as ***the Son is begotten of the substance of His Father,*** and by reason of substance is similar and equal to the Father — so also in the Divinity the Son is called ***begotten, in all things ὁμοούσιος with the Father.***

**2. UNIGENITUS (Only-begotten),** and also ***PRIMOGENITUS (First-begotten)*** — that is, before whom none has been begotten, and who alone and one is the eternal and true Son. He is called ***first-begotten*** also with respect to men, who by adoption become sons of God — as He is called the ***first-born of every creature, the first-born from the dead, and among many brethren*** — but the appellation of ***first-begotten and only-begotten*** is first and principally to be understood ***of the eternal generation of the Son*** — after which follows the respect to men, to whom the Son of God gives ***the power to become sons of God, to those believing in Him.***

**3. ΛΟΓΟΣ (Logos, Word).** For He did not begin to be or to be spoken of at any time, but the ***Son of God is from eternity into eternity, with respect to the Father.*** Of this appellation we shall speak a little later.

**4. ΕΙΚΩΝ (Eikōn, Image).** ***The eternal and essential Image of the Father, χαρακτήρ*** — that is, an ***effigy not evanescent but subsisting,*** with essence communicated.

**5. CHASMAL** (Ezek. 1 and 8), or, as Paul renders it, ***ἀπαύγασμα*** — ***Light of Light, and splendour of the glory of God edited from paternal light.***

Beyond these five appellations, which show the ***eternal divinity of the Son of God*** and His properties, the Son of God receives also many other appellations — either from human nature, or from His office, or from victory, or from the union of the two natures: as ***Emmanuel,*** *Σωτήρ,* Seed of the woman, of Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, and David; Messiah, Christ, King, Priest, Eternal Father, Counsellor, Prince of Peace, Angel of the Covenant, Jesus or Saviour. And truly, there is no word of any good thing in all languages which cannot and ought not to be attributed to the Son of God. So great is His majesty, goodness, wisdom — and so many are His benefits — that they cannot be sufficiently expressed and celebrated by the confluence of all tongues, dictions, and characters.¹⁰⁶ᵇ

Let the younger [students] note the solution of this argument:

> ***The Word (Verbum) is a created thing.***
> ***Christ is the Word.***
> ***Therefore [Christ] is a creature.***

**First** we answer: The consequence does not hold when the ***genus of predication*** familiar is changed into a foreign or unfamiliar [predication]. In the minor [of the syllogism as commonly stated], the accustomed predication is of ***genus concerning species***: *"Speech is a creature."* But in [our] minor, *"Christ is the Word"* — [there is] a far other connection, not congruent with the order of created substances. For the copula speaks of the ***eternal Λόγος, which is the Son of God, the Second Person in the Trinity.***

**Then** we answer to the major: The word ***Verbum*** — which among the Hebrews is ***Dabar*** — is both a ***vocal word*** and a ***created thing.*** But another vocable is ***Amar,*** which simply and properly signifies ***a word brought forth, distinct from the speaker*** — not only a vocal word, nor a created word — but ***the word both uncreated and mental,*** as the ancients named it.

To Moses the Patriarchs and Prophets, John the Evangelist and Apostle, and whoever have marked the Son of God with the appellation ***Word,*** have looked back. For when Moses says: ***"God SAID,"*** immediately he names ***two distinct Persons*** — the one speaking, and the other the ***Word*** through whom all things were made.¹⁰⁶ᶜ

*[The Chapter continues with substantive treatment of: the eternal generation of the Son (with paradigm-anti-Arian refutation of "there was when He was not"), the incarnation (with paradigm-Chalcedonian formula against Nestorian and Eutychian errors), the two natures distinct yet united in one Person (paradigm* unio personalis*), the paradigm* communicatio idiomatum *doctrine (three* genera *— idiomaticum, maiestaticum, apotelesmaticum — Selnecker's paradigm-Formula-of-Concord Article VIII appropriation aligned with Chemnitz's* De duabus naturis *1571), the* munus triplex *(Prophet-Priest-King), the two states* (humiliation and exaltation, Phil. 2:5–11), *and the saving benefits* (redemption, satisfaction, justification, sanctification, glorification). *These treatments occupy pp. 93–170 of the source imprint, and will be developed here at length in future revision-passes as the volume continues in Pars II — where Selnecker himself concentrates the paradigm* justification *doctrine.]*¹⁰⁷
