# Locus III

## De Creatione — On Creation¹¹⁰

*(pp. 258–271 of the imprint.)*

#### Definition of Creation

**Creation of things is properly an external action of God, by which God the eternal Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, together with the eternal Son and the Holy Spirit, willing, first produced from nothing all other things — visible and invisible, spiritual, celestial, corporeal, and terrestrial — of His immense goodness and wisdom — with this counsel: that He might found for Himself an eternal Church, by which He might be acknowledged and worshipped, and to whom He might communicate His own goodness in all eternity.**¹¹⁰ᵃ

**The efficient cause is God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.** For creation is the work of the whole Trinity, not only of one Person — as above in the rule of Augustine ***on works ad extra*** has been shown, and as it is written in **Psalm 33:** ***"By the Word of the Lord were the heavens established, and by the Spirit of His mouth all the host of them."***

That, however, in the Apostles' Symbol the Father is named ***Creator of heaven and earth*** — is done either by reason of ***order*** or ***ministry,*** or by some other difference of cause. For since the Father is the ***first Person,*** and creation is ***the first work*** by which God has manifested Himself to us — and to the Son is attributed the ***work of redemption,*** to the Holy Spirit truly ***sanctification*** (as to Persons sent from the Father, and revealing both the Father and themselves to us) — deservedly to the Father, as the first Person, the first work is attributed: in which He principally manifested Himself, and afterwards did not appear ***in His own hypostasis,*** but sent the Son and the Holy Spirit — through whom He has manifested and announced His essence and will to every age and posterity.¹¹⁰ᵇ

#### The Species of Creatures

**The species of creatures are all created substances, incorporeal and corporeal.** ***Incorporeal,*** as all Angels, both good and evil, the soul of man either in the body or separated from the body — that is, the ***manes*** (souls of the deceased).

***Corporeal*** either ***simple***, as ***ether***, with its nine spheres (as the artificers name them) — namely the ***primum mobile***, and the ***firmament*** which contains the stars (of which ***Aratus*** wrote in Greek), and the ***seven orbs of the planets***; item, the ***four elements***; — or ***compounded and concrete***, and these either ***imperfect,*** as all ***meteors***, or ***perfect***, which are things both inanimate and animate. ***Inanimate*** are either ***produced by nature***, as stones, metals, and liquids, or ***elaborated by art***, as garments, arms, organs, vessels. ***Animate*** either ***non-sentient***, as plants, trees, shrubs, herbs; or ***sentient*** — as an animal — as man [***rational sentient***], as brutes [***irrational sentient***] — terrestrial, aquatic, aerial, etc.

**Creature is defined in this manner: A creature is the whole workmanship of God — as much of spirits as of other corporeal natures — divinely fashioned to this end: that they may show forth that God is, and that He is to be acknowledged and celebrated as ***potent, wise, and good Architect, Creator, Governor, and Conservator.***¹¹⁰ᶜ

#### The Fourfold Consideration of Created Things

**Created things are considered in a fourfold manner:**

**First,** by reason of the ***variety of species*** — which both is most beautiful, and shows the supreme wisdom and power of the workman.

**Secondly,** by reason of the ***propriety of faculties and virtues*** in things produced — which shows the supreme goodness of God, who has founded all things for certain uses. Hence also the physicists say: ***"Nature does nothing in vain, neither abounds in superfluous things, nor is deficient in necessary things."*** Item: ***"Nature acts for the sake of an end"*** — that is, by the counsel of the architect the species are ordered in the whole of nature and are preserved, and have certain forms and effects; and in each single mixed species there is a certain mode of propagation. And ***Aristotle*** rightly says: ***"That which nature does, is according to order."***

**Thirdly,** created things are considered by reason of ***order,*** which is twofold — namely, the ***order of creation,*** and the ***order of things created.*** The order of creation shows that all things have been founded ***for the sake of men,*** as the Stoics rightly said, and that God has ***provided for us all things in His own way before we were born***: for man is the ***end of all things,*** who is more excellent than certain other things destined [to lower ends]. Whence also ***Ovid*** most learnedly says:

> ***"An animal more sacred than these, more capacious of high thought,***
> ***Was yet lacking, and one that could rule the rest.***
> ***Man was born; and while the other animals bend their gaze upon the earth,***
> ***He gave to man an upright face, and bade him behold the sky,***
> ***And lift his gaze erect unto the stars."***

Which words Ovid seems to have borrowed from ***Cicero,*** who in his first book *De Legibus* writes thus: ***"Nature gave the shape of the body suitable and apt to the human genius: for when she had cast down the other animals to their food, she raised up man alone, and as it were roused him by the sight of his ancient kinship and pristine domicile of heaven."*** The same sentiment ***Silius Italicus*** thus renders:

> ***"Dost thou not see how God has raised up the lofty countenances of men***
> ***unto the stars, and fashioned sublime mouths,***
> ***while the herds and the race of birds, and the shapes of wild beasts***
> ***He has bent to the sluggish and obscene belly?"***¹¹⁰ᵈ

It is fitting also to add the words of ***Cicero*** from Book 2 *De Natura Deorum*: ***"All things,"*** he says, ***"have been generated for the sake of others — as fruits and produce, which the earth generates, for the sake of animals; animals moreover for the sake of men — as the horse for carrying, the ox for ploughing, the dog for hunting and guarding; and man himself has arisen for the sake of contemplating and imitating the world."***

**But the order of things created consists in four grades**, of which the ***lowest*** is that of things which ***only are***, as the elements and all mineral things — stones, metals, and the like. The ***second grade*** is of things which ***are and only live*** — that is, are generated, increased, and nourished, but have a ***vegetative soul only***, as plants, herbs, shrubs. The ***third grade*** is of things which ***are, live, and feel,*** as brute animals. The ***fourth order*** is of things which not only ***are, live, feel, desire, and are moved,*** but ***also understand***, as men.

**This order is diligently to be considered,** because it shows that ***by God's counsel the lower grades serve the higher*** — with each one's dignity preserved. The elements serve plants, herbs, brutes, men. Plants serve brutes and men. Brutes serve men. But man — whom should he serve? God the Creator; whom if man does not serve, he now does contrary to his own nature — and inverts, nay, ***perverts,*** the order of nature.¹¹⁰ᵉ

A good rule ***Aristotle*** has laid down concerning the grades of living, saying: ***"Just as prior figures*** (that is, those which are named from an earlier and lesser number of angles) ***are enclosed in later*** (which have their name from a later and greater number of angles) — as the trigon in the tetragon, the tetragon in the pentagon — item, the ternary in the quaternary, and the quaternary in the quinary: ***so prior powers are enclosed in later***: that is, they concur as parts to their constitution, and are consummated by the later — that is, they grow, are increased, are perfected, and are received into the service of the constitution of the whole." **Since therefore all prior things by order and by nature concur to the utility of man, and this natural order seems as it were to consist in this — whom man ought to look upon, in whom he himself is enclosed? Here God the Creator, and nothing else occurs, whom man ought to serve.**

The consideration of this order admonishes us of ***four greatest things***:

**First, of the excellence of man,** by which man excels above all things placed below his order.

**Secondly, of the goal of our actions,** to which we ought to direct and refer all our actions — namely, to the ***glorifying of God***, and to giving Him ***thanks for this benefit*** — that He has founded all things for our utility.

**Thirdly, of the perversity and misery of our nature and of our whole life** — that we are far distant from the dignity and office of our order, so that almost no creature has so fallen out of its own order as we have fallen out of ours. For how few are there who in all things [***make of God***, and refer all things to the glory of God]?

**Fourth, the consideration of our order admonishes us to seek a Mediator of our nature — who is the ***Son of God-Mediator***.**¹¹⁰ᶠ

**Lastly, created things are considered by reason of the ***principal end*** — which from the order of creatures can easily be understood.** For ***all things have been founded for the sake of man; but man for the sake of God*** — not that God needs our service, but that we, serving God, may enjoy the benefits of God. For ***"all things are ours"*** (1 Cor. 3) — namely, as ***Augustine*** narrates: ***higher, equal, and lower*** things. ***Higher*** [things] for enjoyment, as God Himself. ***Equal*** [things] for co-living, as the Angels — who now also serve us, and in eternal life we shall be equal to them. ***Lower*** [things] for use, by their service.

#### Bernard's Seven-fold Consideration of the Benefit of Creation

**Bernard's words are worthy of memory. Consider seven things in the benefit of creation:**

**1.** That He created thee ***not for His own convenience.***
**2.** ***Without any merit of thine,*** because he has merited nothing who was utterly nothing.
**3.** Consider ***of what quality*** He has made [thee].
**4.** ***As to the body,*** an excellent creature.
**5.** ***As to the soul,*** more excellent — as one signed with the image of the Creator, participant of reason, capable of eternal blessedness.
**6.** Consider the ***power of dominion.*** For the terror of man is seen to be poured out upon all living things.
**7.** Consider the ***administration of largesse.*** How many things He has bestowed on thee for sustenance, how many for erudition, how many for consolation, how many for correction, how many for delight? Why should you not give thanks?

**Let these things said in general concerning creation suffice.**¹¹⁰ᵍ

#### From What Are Things Created — The Ex Nihilo Framework

**But it is asked from whence things were founded.** We know from Sacred Scripture that ***no matter out of which*** [pre-existed], but that all things ***in the beginning*** (that is, when there was plainly nothing except the eternal God Himself) were ***made by God from nothing*** — as it is written **Psalm 33:** ***"He Himself spoke, and they were made; He commanded, and they were created."*** And **Ecclesiasticus 18:** ***"He who liveth for ever, created all things together."***¹¹⁰ʰ

#### The Erroneous Opinions of the Philosophers on the Principles of Things

**The philosophers disputed in various manners of the principles of all things,** and thought that ***some certain matter*** from which things had arisen was ***always.***

**Heraclitus,** the ***Obscure,*** around the year of the world's founding 3506, taught that ***fire*** was truly the principle, from which all things consist.

**Diogenes,** in the year of the world 3569, taught that ***air*** was the principle of things.

**Thales the Milesian,** the first Physicist by profession, in the year of the world 3326, said that ***all things were made from water,*** and again were resolved into water — to which opinion alluding, Homer says: [***"Ocean is the origin of all things"***]. Whence the Latins also call ***water aqua***, as it were ***from which*** (*aqua*) all things are born.

**Democritus,** in the year of the world 3462, established ***atoms,*** which he said were as certain seeds, or minutest corpuscles — such that they do not admit ***section,*** and volitating through the void, [contended] that by their casual concurrence and conglobation other bodies are born, and thence were created fire, air, water, earth — and from these the rest. Whence **Virgil** in the *Silenus* says:

> ***"For he sang, how through the great void***
> ***the seeds of lands, air, sea, were driven together;***
> ***and how from these first beginnings***
> ***of light-flowing fire were the primordia,***
> ***and the tender globe of the world itself grew together."***

**Empedocles,** in the year of the world 3509, taught that ***all things are procreated from the four elements.*** Hence **Lucretius:**

> ***"From rain, earth, and breath, and fire, things are born."***

This was also formerly the opinion of the ***physicians.***

**The Stoics** feigned two eternal things: ***God, or Mind, and Nature, or Matter*** — as also ***Hermes Trismegistus the Egyptian,*** who says: [***"Two eternal principles"***].

In the same way the ***Poets*** also established ***two principles, God and Chaos*** — as is clear from Ovid, who imitating Hesiod calls ***Chaos*** the confusion of all things, and a mass compacted into one, distinct and distributed into no species, and not placed in its own place.

**Plato,** in the year of the world 3572, following the Stoics, added moreover an ***Idea***, and said there were ***three principles*** of all things: ***God, Matter, and Idea*** or image of the work in the divine mind — according to which form is sculpted out. He affirmed that the world was thence founded. For in the ***Phaedo*** he writes that ***Mind is the architect of the world.*** And there stands a passage in the ***Epinomis*** which ***Budaeus*** greatly esteemed and admired: [***"The Word, that is, He who is most divine of all, ordered the world; whom, by knowing, the blessed man is affected first by admiration, then by love"***] — that is, ***the WORD, [Logos]*** — He who is most divine of all — ordered the world; whom by knowing, the blessed man is affected first by admiration, then by love ***of thoroughly investigating***: that so he may lead the best and most happy life, and after death may be received into the places suited to his virtue. **That Plato spoke these things concerning the Son of God, Budaeus altogether affirms** — especially since he admonishes Hermias, Erastus, and Coriscus in a certain epistle that they should ***piously venerate*** and ***with all zeal and piety worship the God of all things — Guide and Author of things present and future — through whom, namely, the Father [is to be known and named]***. These things Plato took from the Philosophy of the Hebrews, especially since in Egypt (as Justin, Clement, and others attest) he had studiously come to know the writings of Moses and of some Prophets — whose doctrine and truth, nevertheless, hindered perhaps by fear, he did not freely dare to profess. For thus of him ***Justin*** writes in his hortatory oration to the Gentiles: ***"Plato seems indeed to acknowledge the doctrine of Moses and of the other Prophets concerning the one true God, which he had learned in Egypt — but on account of Socrates's case, fearing lest against him also some Anytus or Meletus should arise as accuser who might exclaim before the Athenian people: 'Plato by his curiosity violates the deities whom the state worships — he does not deem them to be Gods!' — from fear of the hemlock he variously figures his speech about the Gods, giving material both to deniers and affirmers, as one may easily know from his own sayings."***¹¹⁰ⁱ

I shall add the words of Justin pertaining to this: ***"Plato, when after God he makes matter and Idea principles of things, seems to have taken occasion from no other source than from Moses — although he learned the name of species or Idea from Moses's writings, yet had not heard from those experienced in them that no certain knowledge of these is to be acquired save by mystical contemplation. For Moses writes that God spoke thus to him from the tabernacle: 'And thou shalt make me a tabernacle in all things like the exemplar which was shown to thee prescribed on the mountain.' And a little later: 'Thus, in every case, thou shalt make it after the form which I showed thee on the mountain.' Having gained these writings, Plato, after he had diligently pondered them as he ought, thought there was a certain more ancient species, separated from that which is perceived by sense — which he was also wont to name the exemplar of things, because the letters of Moses so speak concerning the tabernacle: 'After the species shown to thee on the mountain, thou shalt make it.' In the same manner concerning earth, heaven, and man he seems to have slipped into error: for he thought that of these also there were Ideas. Moses writes: 'In the beginning God made heaven and earth': then he subjoins: 'But the earth was rude and invisible.' This the philosopher so understood: 'the earth was' — said of a more ancient earth. And what Moses said, 'God made heaven and earth,' he thought was said of a sensible earth made after the form or species of that more ancient one. Item, of the created heaven — that another had been made, which is called firmament, and this sensible; but that the other was intelligible, of which that vates says: 'The heaven of heaven is the Lord's, but the earth He hath given to the sons of men.' The same happened with man — whom Moses first names, and afterwards, after many creatures, recounts how he was fashioned from mud, saying: 'And God made man, taking mud from the earth.' Plato therefore thought that this first-named man was more ancient than the one created here — but the latter fashioned from mud after the pattern of that species."*** Thus far Justin on Plato's opinion.¹¹⁰ʲ

#### Aristotle on the Eternity of the World

**Aristotle,** in the year of the world 3600, taught that ***the world is eternal,*** and that ***three principles are simultaneously perpetual*** — matter, form, and privation in elementary matter — deceived by physical arguments taken from this order of nature already founded: ***"From nothing, nothing is made."*** Whence he said that ***the first and last man cannot be given.*** Item: that the ***universe neither arose nor is corrupted, but is one and sempiternal — having no beginning or end of eternity.***

What pertains to the physical axiom ***"from nothing, nothing is made"*** — it is to be known that this holds ***in nature already founded and ordered***, and pertains to the ***generation of created things*** — not truly to the ***first creation of things founded from nothing.***

**For creation differs from physical generation.** Creation is the work ***of God alone***, and is understood as ***from a non-being to being*** — such that no matter pre-exists. **Physical generation** is the motion ***from non-being to being*** — that is, from ***not-such or not-of-that-kind*** being to ***such*** being — and has place in a thing already created by God, as also does corruption, of which it is said: ***"Something is not resolved into nothing."*** For corruption is from ***being to non-being*** — that is, from ***such*** being to ***not-such*** being. **Annihilation, however, is the work of God alone** — ***from something-being to plainly-nothing-being*** — such that both matter and form are destroyed.¹¹⁰ᵏ

**And although Aristotle in the eighth book of his *Physics* endeavours by many arguments to confirm his opinion on the eternity of the world**, nevertheless of all those [arguments] only ***two*** are of any moment — which, once understood and refuted, the remainder fall of themselves. These are:

> ***"A proper and most perfect cause is not idle in eternity.***
> ***God is a proper and most perfect cause.***
> ***Therefore He is not idle."***

He would however have been idle from eternity, unless the world had always existed as a ***subject*** in which He could exercise His powers.

**The response is easy — first to the major.** The major is true of ***natural efficients***, not of ***voluntary*** — as fire applied to stubble cannot but burn, but the smith can suspend his action. Since this is true of all voluntary [agents], how much more is it true of the ***first cause*** — which is the ***fount and origin*** of every voluntary and free faculty, and acts deliberately and freely, and most freely at a certain time founded the world.

**But what is inquired — ***"What did God do in that eternity before the world was founded?"*** — the response in the Church is easy: namely, that ***the communication of wisdom between God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit was eternal*** — especially since the Son of God from eternity ***is*** and is named [***the Word***]. What beyond this we have to answer, we have not. For neither do we understand what ***eternity*** is, nor do we attain what things are ***outside time*** and ***before time,*** nor can we comprehend God in His eternal and essential light and rest. Therefore ***Augustine*** says that he here contracts the sails of his own ingenuity. He adds also that when a certain smatterer inquired ***what God had done before the creation***, [Augustine] replied that ***God was fabricating a hell for such questioners***. Whence verses long ago written by us are recited:

> ***"Thou askest, Father Augustine, what He did before***
> ***the beginning, by which all things founded are, God did.***
> ***Thou repliest that He was fabricating a hell for such questioners,***
> ***that they may deservedly suffer the punishments they have merited.***
> ***Say to the auditor: Whoever seizes upon the mysteries of the Word***
> ***with feeble genius, perishes."***

**Of these and similar questions Epiphanius against Aetius speaks thus:** ***"The ratiocinations of men are miserable, and their thoughts corruptible, and they involve themselves, and are transferred into syllogisms and questions. For some out of their own ratiocinations, by a certain sophistical [approach], investigate concerning the origin of evil; others, whence the devil is, and why he was made; others, why God created man to sin; others, why God should ascribe fault to man, when He had created such a one that could sin. When they have moved all their thoughts by these questions, let them say themselves to be corruptible, and attribute to God alone knowledge and adoration, and let them ask and obtain from Him the true knowledge of faith — let them not think too highly of themselves, but think unto this, that they may be modest,"*** as it is written (Rom. 12), [***"not thinking more highly than one ought to think, but thinking unto modesty"***].¹¹⁰ˡ

#### Distinctions of Terms — Aeternitas, Aevum, Tempus

**Let us also know that we, who yet are and live in time, cannot understand the ratio of eternity.** For God ***founded time*** together with created things — nor was there time before there was heaven and earth, but eternity. Whence Augustine, *De Civitate Dei* book 12, chapter 15, and *De Trinitate* book 5, chapter 6, says: ***"God was before time was, and He did not begin in time to be Lord: because He was Lord of time when time began to be — nor indeed did time begin to be in time, since time was not before time began."***

**Let the younger observe the distinctions of terms:**

***Aeternitas*** (Eternity) is properly attributed only to God, who lacks beginning and end.

***Aevum*** (Age) is called the duration of those things which have begun to be at a certain beginning, but do not cease, as the Angels and the souls of men.

***Tempus*** (Time) is the duration of changeable things which have beginning and end — as all corporeal things serving man in this life.

Hence our own verses are recited:

> ***"That which began, time also, ceases to be,***
> ***And with swift foot hastens to the supreme goal.***
> ***Age had beginning, but never ceases —***
> ***Thus the Angelic choir itself beholds the Light.***
> ***But God is the eternal Preserver and Author of things,***
> ***And without beginning is He, and without end — God."***¹¹⁰ᵐ

**What Jerome in his letter to Titus says** — ***"Six thousand years of our time are not yet fulfilled; and how great eternities before, how great times, how great origins of ages must be conjectured to have been — in which Angels, Thrones, Dominions, and the other orders served God without alternations of time and measures, and by God's command sustained themselves"*** — that we leave [***as belonging to his own place***] to that place [***as an unknown and unresolved matter***], and we use the words of Augustine which he wrote to Jerome himself, saying: ***"My brother, I do not think that thou wouldst have thy books read as though they were of Prophets or Apostles — of whose books to doubt that they lack all error is impious."*** And Jerome himself in the same letter to Titus says: ***"Without the authority of the Scriptures garrulity has no faith."*** Item: ***"Let sense be exercised, and let the mind be nourished daily by divine reading — and our questions will not be foolish."***

#### The Second Aristotelian Argument — Perpetual Motions

**The second argument of Aristotle is this:** That which does not change, is not subject to generation and corruption. The laws of celestial motions do not change, but are [***invariable and always in the same manner***]. Therefore this mass always was — and, thus always circumacted, has effected the various alternations of generations and corruptions in inferior things.

**The response is plain and easy.** The argument is ***particular*** — and it cannot be said of the whole world and of all its parts, which certainly are ***subject to alterations***. Moreover, the argument does not hold from physical generation to perpetuity. For creation is to be distinguished from physical generation, as has been said above.

**These are the chief arguments by which human reason is disturbed. But let students read the ***refutation of some Aristotelian dogmas*** by ***Justin the philosopher and martyr***, and consider these words of Justin: ***"The Greek professors,"*** he says, ***"do not speak of God and of creation according to demonstrative science, but define by conjectures whatever seems to them, and commit the investigation of the knowledge of God to their own ratiocinations; and they greatly disagree, both among themselves and from themselves, on things and their principles — as to essence, number, motion, and end."***

**Let us therefore assent to the divine voice** — that is, to the writings of Moses and of the Prophets, who, as Justin truly and piously says, ***learned from the Creator God Himself the doctrine of God and of creation.***¹¹⁰ⁿ

#### The Chronology of the World

**Let us also know that in this year of Christ's birth 1571,** the years from the world's founding are ***5533*** — and let us not seek a longer space, and let us know that we already live in the ***old age*** [***nay, in the belly and dregs***] of the world, and expect the coming of the Son of God.

**Let us also hold the saying of Elias:** ***"Six thousand years shall the world endure. Two thousand [years] in vain; two thousand under the Law; two thousand [in the] days of the Messiah."*** And if anything of these shall be lacking, it shall be lacking on account of our sins, which are many and innumerable.

That today many count the ***seventh millennium from the founding of the world*** — as is often read in certain vulgar Calendars — has happened by error. For the ***Seventy Interpreters,*** at Gen. 5 and 11, often rendered ***two hundred*** for ***one hundred*** — perhaps taking the singular noun ***Meah*** (hundred) for the plural noun ***Meoth*** — which signifies ***a hundred repeated many times*** — or for the dual ***Meothim*** (two hundred). The excessive years in the Seventy have afterwards begotten many errors, as is detected in the *Chronicon* of Eusebius, in Epiphanius, and in many others. And Jerome also admonishes of this error in the *Hebrew Questions.*¹¹⁰ᵒ

#### Closing Recommendation for the Student

**But of creation let students read Moses himself, and Psalms 104 and 136 — item, the *Hexaemeron* of ***Basil the Great*** — a work stuffed with erudition and stupendous piety.**

**Now of the Angels we shall add a brief admonition.**¹¹⁰ᵖ

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

# Locus IV

## De Angelis — On the Angels¹¹²

*(pp. 271–283 of the imprint.)*

**1. That there are Angels, both good and evil, both Holy Scripture and experience attest.**

**2. That they are created by God, and that they are Spirits,** Ps. 104 attests: ***"Who makest Thine Angels spirits, and Thy ministers a flame of fire."*** And Moses at the beginning of the second chapter [of Genesis]: ***"God finished all His work which He had made"*** — includes the creation of the Angels also, whom we name ***Maleachim.*** That, however, Moses expressly says nothing of the ***creation or fall of the Angels*** — he undoubtedly does with peculiar and altogether grave counsel. Moses wished to entrust to letters those things whose knowledge would be necessary to us for life, doctrine, consolation, and salvation. All others he omitted, lest he should give occasion to curious [minds] — whether Jews or others — to indulge in questions and speculations, to which human reason in this life after the fall is neither equal nor able to satisfy — and whose knowledge is to be deferred, until we have been received into the ***eternal Academy.***

That the Angels are created by God is in no wise doubted. ***When*** they were created is disputed, but not investigated — except that we know they were created ***not before the world was founded*** (since it is written: *"In the beginning"* — before which, namely, no creature was: this is the beginning of creatures) — but ***on the first day,*** as is very consonant and probable. Certainly in the creation of all things it is established that they too were established. ***How*** they were created is not difficult to know — namely, ***both spirits and good.*** For they are spiritual natures, as Ps. 104 attests. That all were originally created good is attested by this single saying: ***"God saw all things which He had made, and they were very good."*** And Christ manifestly says (John 8): ***"In the truth the Devil hath not remained."*** He was therefore in the beginning created ***good,*** yet did not remain in his goodness.

**3. How many Angels there are — inquiry is not needed.** But that they are ***many, and by our reason innumerable,*** is attested by: (a) that Angels are called ***the hosts of the Lord***; (b) that Elisha (2 Kings 6) says: ***"More are they that be with us, than they that be with them"***; (c) Ps. 68: ***"The chariots of God are twenty thousand"***; (d) that the Son of God Himself (Matt. 26) affirms that ***twelve legions of Angels*** will come to His help if only He wills — and one legion consists of ten cohorts, that is, 6,660 [approximately] — so twelve legions constitute an immense number of Angels; (e) that each individual man has his own ***guardian Angel*** — as Jerome piously says: ***"Great is the dignity of souls, that each has, from the moment of birth, an Angel deputed for his guard."*** And Origen: ***"There is present with each one of us — even the least, who are in the Church of God — a good Angel, an Angel of the Lord, who rules, admonishes, governs, and daily sees the face of the Father who is in heaven for the correction of our acts and the exhortation of our mercies."*** (Matt. 18; cf. Apoc. 5.)

**4. Formerly Monks and others constituted the nine orders of Angels** from the ***Celestial Hierarchy*** of pseudo-Dionysius, which they wished to obtrude upon the simpler as if they themselves had been in heaven. Since there are three ***estates*** in the human race — Ecclesiastical, Political, and Economic — they assigned three Angels to each estate, who fight with the assailing Devils against men placed in one of these estates and hinder them.¹¹³

But let the younger [students] observe the significations of the ***appellations***:

- **Seraphim** — from *"burning,"* namely with love of God and of the Church, or that they are of a luminous nature (as the Hebrews interpret Maleachi ach = Angels flashing, like fire — Ps. 104: *"Thou makest Thy ministers as a flame of fire, coruscation and lightning"*). These are mentioned in Isa. 6, where the Seraphim sing: ***"Holy, holy, holy is the Lord of hosts; the whole earth is full of His glory."*** And Ezekiel 28 calls them ***fiery stones.***
- **Cherubim** — ***human Angels,*** having the appearance of man (as the Rabbis say — that is, appearing in the form of man), with wide and open forehead, or with a florid and youthful face, ruddy mouth, and beautiful cheeks — depicted with wings.

- **Thrones (Θρόνοι), seats of God.** Their appellations are taken from Paul, **Colossians 1** — who at **Eph. 6** uses the same [names] also of the evil spirits, where he joins together ***[principalities, powers, rulers of this world of darkness].***

**There stand also in Scripture certain names of Angels: Michael, Raphael, Gabriel, Uriel.**

**Michael** the Archangel — ***[Who is like unto God?]*** — mention of him is made **Dan. 10, 12; Rev. 12,** in the Epistle of Jude. And beyond doubt it is the ***Son of God Himself*** who is set before us in this appellation, who is the ***Great Prince standing for His people*** (Dan. 12), and sends other Angels also fighting for us against the evil spirits (Dan. 10); whence Malachi 3 calls the Son of God the ***Angel of the covenant desired.*** For He is the paradigm-***legate and messenger [of the Father to men, the Angel of great counsel, sent unto the human race for its redemption].***

**Raphael** — ***the Physician of God*** — in the history of Tobit. **Gabriel** — ***the Strength of God*** (for this is the name of the Angel who expounded to Daniel the vision and prophecy of the seventy weeks, Dan. 8, 9; and announces to Mary the conception of Christ, and to Zacharias the nativity of John, Luke 1). **Uriel** — ***the fire or splendour of God*** — 4 Esdras 4.

Of the appellations of the evil spirits we shall speak a little later. Now let us consider the offices of the good Angels.¹¹³ᵃ

#### The Six Offices of the Good Angels

**4. For what end the good Angels are created by God, Theodoret elegantly signifies by two words: [ὑμνῳδοῦσι καὶ διακονοῦσι] — that is, ***they celebrate God with hymns, and minister*** — as the Epistle to the Hebrews names the Angels [***λειτουργικὰ πνεύματα εἰς διακονίαν ἀποστελλόμενα διὰ τοὺς μέλλοντας κληρονομεῖν σωτηρίαν***] — ***"ministering spirits sent forth for the sake of those who shall inherit salvation."*** For teaching's sake these offices can be enumerated in order.

**First, the Angels are patrons and defenders of truth** — that is, they seek the glory of God, and defend and propagate the true doctrine of the righteousness and salvation of men, defend the Word of God, help and protect pious teachers and hearers, hinder the Devil's endeavours, restrain the fury and audacity of all who deprave the Word of God, and remove and take away false teachers and heretics, as Cerinthus, Ebion, Arius, and countless others, even in our own age. They also retain the pious lest they rush into manifest errors or be seduced; and often draw back the seduced — as Eusebius reports of Natalius, that he was scourged the whole night by Angels, when he had begun to be moved by heretics to deny Christ's divinity.

**Secondly, the Angels are legates and messengers of God to revelations of things unknown to human reason** — yet consenting with the promise and Word of God — sent [***by God***]. For therefore are they called Angels ***from office,*** since ***by nature*** they are Spirits. Examples are Dan. 9, 10, 11, 12; Luke 1, 2. Such revelations were frequent in the Old Testament up to the incarnation of the Son of God — who, since He has manifested and brought forth to us from the bosom of the eternal Father all things which for our consolation, life, and salvation are needful, wills that we ***acquiesce and be content with His own Word*** — so that we do not need new visions and revelations, nor ought we to seek them — especially since our nature in this old age of the world has become more languid and infirm than that it could bear the sight and visible familiarity of the most excellent Spirits. We have the Word, and we know that the Angels of God are with us. For these benefits let us be grateful to God, and not tempt Him by soliciting new revelations.

**There yet live some men eminent in piety and doctrine** who heard from Luther that ***he could easily have obtained a vision of an Angel from God in his own perils, had he wished it*** — and by this persuasion he was not deceived, but always feared to tempt God, since he had the expressed Word of God — of which it is written: ***"If even an Angel from heaven should teach another Gospel, let him be anathema."*** And therefore the Word was more certain and more dear to him than if he had had all revelations, in which he might also have been deceived — since the Devil's craft is by no means to be believed, who can turn himself into an Angel of light, and appear as though he were sent from God — as in the *Lives of the Fathers,* as they call them, a memorable example is read: namely, that demons came in the appearance of Angels to a certain pious man, and one of them larger than the rest, conspicuous as a king with golden crown and other ornaments, said: ***"Behold, O man, all things thus far which are pleasing to me thou hast performed, nor doth anything else remain, save that thou now adore me here speaking before thee — then I shall transform thee, as Elijah [was translated]."*** To whom he: ***"What this may be, I know not — since I daily adore Christ my Lord, and He requires from me no other form of adoration, for He knows Himself to be adored by me indefectibly: wherefore begone and depart, Satan; I have and adore a king — thou art not my king."*** With these words spoken, the spectre is read to have vanished.

There is also reported this — which is to be observed by children — that the Devil appeared transformed into an Angel of light and said to someone: ***"I am Gabriel the Angel, and I have been sent to thee"***: to whom he: ***"See that thou be sent to another, for I am not worthy that an Angel should be despatched to me, since I live in sins."*** Item: that Satan came to a certain old man, and said: ***"I am Christ; do not shut thine eyes"***: to whom the elder: ***"I do not wish to see Christ here, but in another life."*** That such things came to pass in the Papacy and in monasteries there is no doubt, and some old men remember examples.

**Thirdly, the Angels are defenders of the whole Church** — in doctrine against heretics, and in all perils against tyrants, malevolent men, sycophants, and flatterers. Wherefore they are rightly named ***guardians and holy watchers*** (Dan. 4). And here belongs the whole history of the going forth from Egypt — Exod. 12 (where all the firstborn are killed); Exod. 13 (where by day the pillar of cloud, by night the pillar of fire leads the people); Exod. 14 (where the Angel of the Lord suffocates Pharaoh and the Egyptian army with the waters). Item the history of Elisha (2 Kings 6), who, when he was besieged by the army of the King of Syria, marvellously through the army of Angels obtains victory. And Isa. 37 — the Angel slays the army of Sennacherib and delivers King Hezekiah. Dan. 10 — the Angel resists the demonic prince-of-the-court of the kingdom of Persia. For kingdoms and courts have their great cacodaemons — whom if the good Angels did not resist, empires would collapse, nor would any good remain — as by our little verses it is rightly wont to be said:

> ***"By how much any state is greater, by how much any power greater,***
> ***By so much are the ambushes of the demon greater."***

**In the same way the Angels have restrained Herod (Acts 12), Nero, Julian, and other tyrants** — and today marvellously and powerfully hold back the Turk and other enemies, lest they make onslaught upon the wretched Church, today also contemned by her own — who ought to be so called — nurses, and deserted. For it would go badly with us, if — supported by the aid of leaders alone — our Germany were destitute of the fidelity of the Angels, which is manifestly beheld and grasped in so great sloth, security, discord, perversity, and degeneration of the superior [rulers].¹¹³ᵇ

I shall add the history which Josephus recounts in book 11 *[Antiquities],* last chapter. **Alexander the Great,** angry with the Jew Jaddus who denied him help against Tyre and the Persians, came into Judaea to dissipate it. But Jaddus the high priest, adorned with priestly habit, together with the whole crown of priests and elders, went forth to meet Alexander, and suppliantly sought peace. Alexander, immediately upon seeing him, descended from his horse, reverently saluted and received Jaddus, clemently promised peace, and himself became a suppliant to Jaddus. When the leaders were held with admiration of this thing, Alexander said that he in Macedonia before the war had seen in a dream such an appearance of a divinity, adorned in similar habit, by whom he was both called into Asia, and commanded to undertake war against the Persians, and was made certain of obtaining victory: ***"For that appearance extended to me its right hand, and promised defence and success. Since therefore I now see the priest similar to that appearance, I have established that God has care of this people, and that I have been aided by God of this people, and therefore I wish to spare and do well to this people."***

**Well-known also is the history of Attila invading Italy and wishing to devastate it.** For when he was about to approach Rome, there was sent to meet him Leo, Bishop of Rome, and part of the senate, who sought peace and as suppliants prayed that he would spare the city of Rome, which had generated so many excellent men — whose virtue is famous in the whole world. Attila, beyond all opinion of all, obeyed them willingly and gladly. Being asked the cause, he said that he had seen a certain man standing by Leo the Bishop, of appearance more than human, terrible, threatening him death if he did not spare the suppliants.

**Anastasius the Emperor** — who is thought to have perished by lightning — when he had apprehended Justin and Justinian, who afterwards became rulers of affairs, and had wished to slay them, saw in a dream a terrible Man, saying: ***"Do no evil to Justin and Justinian, for both shall serve God in their own time."***

**Fourthly, rightly did Origen say: "Angels are present in our assembly not only generally with the whole Church, but singly with even the least, who are in the Church of God — of whom the Saviour says: 'Their Angels always behold the face of My Father'"*** (Matt. 18). For ***privately they guard the individual pious,*** and defend our life and safety, as Ps. 34 has expressly and emphatically written: ***"The Angel of the Lord encampeth round about them that fear Him."*** On which saying Basil the Great, in Book 3 against Eunomius, says thus: [***"An Angel stands by every one who believes in Christ, unless we drive him away by our sins. For as smoke drives away bees, and stench drives away doves, so tenebrous and repulsive sin drives away the guardian Angel of our life."***] Thus this passage has been alleged by some. The words are these: [***"An Angel accompanies each of those who have believed on the Lord, unless we drive him away by wicked works: for as smoke puts bees to flight, and stench doves, so sin also puts to flight the Angel guardian of life."***] Item: [***"To each one of the faithful an Angel is appointed as a pedagogue and shepherd, directing his life,"***] etc.

There is no doubt that David took the word "to encamp" (*castrametari*) from Genesis 32, where Angels meet the Patriarch Jacob, and thence the place received the name ***Mahanaim***, that is, ***two camps*** — namely of Jacob himself and of the Angels who surrounded and defended the Patriarch. ***"For this is God's troop,"*** says Jacob. And chapter 48 he says: ***"The God who feedeth me from my youth even unto this present day, the Angel who delivereth me from all evils, bless these lads."*** And Ps. 91 David says: ***"He hath commanded His Angels concerning thee, to bear thee up in their hands, lest thou dash thy foot against a stone."***

Well known are the examples: as **Tobit** led by the Angel, defended, healed, delivered. And **Peter** (Acts 12) marvellously led out of prison through an Angel. Item, **Daniel** preserved among the lions. And **Lot** defended from Sodom. And **Christ** Himself says: ***"The Angels of the children behold the face of the heavenly Father"*** — on which passage the verse of Juvencus (who lived 1233 years ago), let children observe:

> ***"Let no one of the loft [proud] scorn these little ones,***
> ***For their guardians in the heavenly seat protect them,***
> ***And behold the face of the high-throned Father above the stars."***¹¹³ᶜ

**But let us recite a few examples of the Angels' guardianship.**

**Sozomen,** book 1, chapter 3 of Church History, writes that **Constantine**, anxious about the war against Maxentius, disputed with himself whom he should have as helper — and in his sleep saw the sign of the cross in a clear sky; and while he was stupefied at its aspect, he heard the voice of Angels standing by him: ***"Constantine, in this sign thou shalt conquer."***

**Socrates,** book 3 of Church History, chapter 19, reports: when at the order of ***Julian the Apostate*** Christians were seized, a certain young man among them, named Theodorus, was subjected to various torments, and lacerated in his whole body — and was not removed from the torments except when it seemed he could no longer see, and yet could by no means be bent to the denying of the truth. When Ruffinus asked him whether he had felt the greatest torment while he was being tortured, he answered that ***his torment from the torments was very slight, for there stood by him a certain young man, who wiped away the sweat that flowed forth from the agony, and corroborated his mind, and made the time of the torture more agreeable and grateful than laborious.***

**The same, book 7, chapter 23** reports the history of Ardaburius, general of the Emperor Theodosius, who by adverse wind fell into the hands of the tyrant, enemy of Theodosius. When the Emperor and others were anxious and implored God's help for Ardaburius, an Angel appearing in the form of a shepherd led the son of Ardaburius and many who were with him through a marsh — through which no one had ever crossed — to Ravenna, where the tyrant was, and [effected] that they should slay the tyrant, that they might bring back Ardaburius safe — which was done.

**In the same place, chapter 18** is recited the history: Angels appeared near Bithynia to Ardaburius and his army waging war against the Persians, and commanded that they should be of good courage, that they should pray, and trust in God — for they were ***dispensers of that war*** sent from God. On hearing this, both the city of Constantinople was strengthened, and the soldiers made more confident.

**The history concerning Simon Grynaeus is known to us,** which happened at Speyer in the year 1529, described by us long ago in these verses:

> ***"It was the time when the affairs of religion were to be debated,***
> ***Here where the famed walls of Nemetes stand.***
> ***And after the cradles of saving Christ***
> ***three hundred and fifteen lustra had been added.***
> ***And twice-seven Phoebus [Sun] had completed those orbs***
> ***by which he traverses the splendid stars of the shining sky.***
> ***Here were present those whose piety, name, and honour***
> ***fill both houses of the ethereal Sun.***
> ***Among them, gleaming with grave virtue, was Philip [Melanchthon],***
> ***concerning whom, who is good, brings back what is best.***
> ***Grynaeus, to see his friends, thus visited those walls***
> ***with his companions, a traveller.***
> ***Behold — a certain one filling the vain temple with clamour,***
> ***Faber, deranged, does mad things and without mind.***
> ***What a mouse chews, gnawing the consecrated bread,***
> ***and by many trifles moves tender hearts.***
> ***Grynaeus hears him speaking in the midst of the temple,***
> ***until the sermon reaches its end.***
> ***Then he follows Faber, addresses him in friendly words,***
> ***but Faber bids him return at set of sun.***
> ***What does he do? The lying delator goes to the king,***
> ***prepares hard chains and ambushes with courtly manners.***
> ***Fearing nothing, Grynaeus in the whiteness of no fraud***
> ***in simplicity, returns to thee, illustrious Philip.***
> ***Behold, at dinner-time suddenly summoned by a voice,***
> ***thou seest thyself, Philip, an old man standing by.***
> ***He says: 'Lictors shall come in ten-fold host,***
> ***they shall lead Grynaeus away, if delay be endured.***
> ***Live long, Philip.' Then with anxious mind Philip***
> ***signifies to his companions that they are now in the meshes.***
> ***They rise, and lead Grynaeus through the midst of the throng***
> ***with anxious hand to the Rhine's flowing streams.***
> ***Here with easy breeze, with wind urging her helpful,***
> ***the little boat by God's mouth cleaves the safe waters.***
> ***When the rest of the guests return to the threshold — behold, misery! —***
> ***they perceive the throng that had scarcely left the house.***
> ***Thus was Faber's craft eluded divinely.***
> ***How well did that Angel — the old man — rule the work!***
> ***Thus daily we are preserved, and an Angel stands by***
> ***who knows how to hold the safe path for us."***

**Known also is the history of the Electors of Saxony Frederick and his brother John,** who, carried in one ship from Torgau to Wittenberg, were in great peril, when the ship was assailed on all sides by waves and ice, so that they now seemed about to perish. When they at length arrived at Wittenberg, immediately as they disembarked, the ship was shaken and broken. The princes stood stupefied on the bank, and saw themselves to have been divinely preserved. Frederick at length said: ***"Let us give thanks to God for His clement guardianship. But I fear lest, we having disembarked, the home and Saxon ship be miserably shaken. Woe is us."***

**In Silesia two memorable histories happened, which show that the Angels bring us aid against the Devils.**¹¹³ᵈ A certain nobleman, when he had invited guests, and the hour of the banquet was already at hand, and the apparatus made — but was frustrated of the guests, who excused themselves why they did not appear — burst forth angrily in these words: ***"Let all the demons come, then, if no one of men can be with me."*** With these words he goes out of the house into the church, where the pastor was giving a sermon, and — to digest his anger — listens for some time. But while he tarries in the temple, come horsemen into the courtyard of the house, great and black, and command the servant of the nobleman to summon his master, and signify to him that ***guests are present.*** The consternated servant goes to the temple, and indicates these things to his master, who — unaware of counsel — consults the pastor. The pastor, interrupting the sermon, gives this counsel: that the whole household leave the house — which was done, when both servants and maids hastened away; but perchance oblivious, the infant lying and sleeping in the cradle they did not take with them. The demons begin to come and go, and to cry out, and to look through the windows in the forms of bears, wolves, cats, and men, and to show goblets filled with wine and food and cups. When the neighbours, the nobleman, the pastor, and others saw these things, the father exclaimed: ***"Where is my infant?"*** Scarcely had he said these things, when one of the demons carries the infant in his arms to the windows, as if to show him to the parents. The nobleman utterly consternated and anxious for the safety of the infant, has a faithful servant whom he asks: ***"What art thou now to do?"*** The servant: ***"Master,"*** he says, ***"I shall commend and commit my life to my God, and in the name of the Lord I shall enter, and I shall see whether by the help and benefit of God I may take away the infant."*** ***"Well,"*** says the nobleman, ***"may God be with thee, and aid thee, and confirm thy mind."*** The servant, receiving the blessing from the pastor and the rest, enters, and before the chamber in which the demons were assembled, falls upon his knees, and commends himself to God — and with this mind opens the door and sees the demons of horrible appearance, sitting, standing, walking about, creeping, and with one onset running to him, and crying out: ***"Ho, ho, what dost thou want here?"*** He praying — and yet divinely confirmed — addresses the demon who carried the infant: ***"Thou,"*** he says, ***"deliver the infant to me."*** ***"By no means,"*** the other responds, ***"the infant is now mine; tell thy master that he come and take the infant."*** To him the servant: ***"I fulfil now the office of my vocation, in which God has placed me, and I know that whatever in this office I do is pleasing to God. Wherefore by reason of my office, and by help, name, and virtue of Jesus Christ, I take from thee the infant, and restore [him] to his father."*** And thus he took hold of the infant, and embraced him with his arms. These [demons] answered nothing else, save crying out: ***"Ho, wretch! Ho, wretch! Leave the infant, leave — or we shall tear thee to pieces."*** He caring nothing for the diabolical threats, departed safe, and restored the infant safe to his noble father. The demons afterwards, some days elapsed, vanished — that the whole family of the nobleman was able to migrate again into the house.

**The other history is:** A certain noble, according to his custom, called all the Devils — and riding sometimes at night had no one with him except a boy. Then a great throng of Devils rushed upon him, as if to lead him away. The boy, desiring his lord's safety, embraces him with his arms. The Devils cry out: ***"Boy, take away thy hands."*** By this reason the nobleman was saved. For the boy had with him the guardian Angels, whose limbs the Devils could not harm. Whence also from that side where the boy sat on horseback, the demons had always withdrawn.

**In Hessia there is a famous family of nobles named Pamelbergica.** A certain one of these, when he had been sent by his princes to enroll soldiers, and had to ride by night through a dense forest, heard demons behind him vexing him, and coming closer by turns — at length two carried a bier before him, and hindered the road. He, in so great peril and terror of mind, invokes God with a loud voice — and while he was doing this, he sees fiery swords by which two men drive away the spectres and demons. A little afterwards he himself died.

**Known is the history of the boy of Schwaben** [Cygneus] who in winter for three days was preserved by an Angel on a very high mountain — so that the boy neither froze nor hungered, nor was there snow in the place where the boy stayed — until at length he was found by his parents. But I cease to commemorate more histories, which experience augments and confirms with a great mass.

**Let us give thanks to God for so great guardians of our bodies, life, and goods.** I therefore add some verses written by me once in the form of a prayer:

> ***"Son of God, since Thou hast broken the neck of the Dragon,***
> ***Subjecting to Thy feet the nefarious monsters,***
> ***Join to us ministers of the Angelic order,***
> ***That they may guard all our limbs and forces.***
> ***When black hunger rages, and pestilence grows heavy everywhere,***
> ***When the shafts and balls of funest Mars fly,***
> ***Surround us with an Angelic troop, that nothing***
> ***Even so little may be able to harm us.***
> ***Ah pious Christ, confirm faith in our breast,***
> ***We are nothing — ah, may Thy hand, Christ, preserve us."***

#### The Fifth and Sixth Offices — Prayer and Consolation at Death

**5. The Angels pray both for the Church and for the members of the Church**, as by these words Christ indicates: ***"Their Angels behold the face of my Father who is in heaven."*** For nothing is more grateful to the Angels than the safety of the Church and the salvation of men — as Christ says (Luke 15): ***"There is joy before the Angels of God over one sinner that repenteth."*** For the Angels rejoice in heaven when the sinner does penance for three causes: (1) because men are with them founded for celebrating God; (2) because they see the kingdom of God augmented by the accession of new citizens; (3) because they desire the destruction of the kingdom of Satan.

Here belongs that which is reported of **Abbot Paul**, who among the rest, who frequented the temple, had seen one both black and deformed of body and vestment, and drawn hither and thither by demons with a bridle placed in his nostrils — the sad and pale Angel following him — but the sermon finished, [had seen] the same [one] with a bright face and white body, and his Angel a cheerful guardian and companion, while the demons at a distance followed. When he asked the same [man] to narrate the cause of this thing to him, he answered: ***"I have now lived many years in sins. Now, entering the Church, I heard the words of Isaiah 1: 'Wash yourselves, be clean, take away malices from your souls before my sight. And if your sins were as scarlet, they shall be made white as snow,' etc. Hearing these things I said, 'Lord, who hast come into the world to save sinners, have mercy on me, and complete in work in me a miserable sinner what through Thy Prophet Thou hast promised,'"*** etc.

**Similar almost is the history said to have happened at Regensburg,** when a certain nobleman, an enemy of the purified doctrine, had often entered the temple — as they called it — of the Lutherans, with a mind to carp at the preacher, and perhaps had heard the explanation of the Decalogue: suddenly he was struck with such tremor and terror that, led from the temple to home, he lay down lethally sick. Recognising his sins, and perceiving the end of life at hand, he ordered the same preacher to be summoned to himself — and, instructed and informed by him in the true acknowledgement and confession of the Son of God, after a few days he died — foretelling beforehand the ignominy with which Satan (whom he had for some time served as a magician) was ultimately to afflict and deform his body placed in the bier — which also was done with great clamour. These things pertain to the illustration of the saying of Isa. 55: ***"My Word shall not return to me void."***

**Thus therefore the Angels pray for the Church, for all the pious, for the conversion and salvation of men, and bear our prayers and good works to God** — as the verses of a certain old Poet express, which I shall subjoin:

> ***"There is that in heaven — more excellent than every number —***
> ***the Angelic choir without end, which with perennial praise***
> ***cries out celebrating the Lord, wont to bear***
> ***serving ministries, and to obey supernal commands.***
> ***These now what right things mortal hearts pray,***
> ***conceive in worthy vows from the holy breast,***
> ***whatever the generous hand scatters upon the gathered needy,***
> ***they receive holy, and bear with holy flight above the stars.***
> ***Moreover the just, while frail life wearies them,***
> ***they protect, and amid the perils of the world they preserve."***

**6. The Angels in the final agony, when life is to be exchanged for death, console us, confirm us, and transport our souls into Abraham's bosom** — as of Lazarus (Luke 16) it is written. And Cyprian in his oration ***De Mortalitate*** recites that an Angel stood by a certain [Christian] already about to expire, and rebuked him because he too greatly feared death, and said: ***"Ye fear to suffer, ye do not wish to depart — what then shall I do with you?"*** — and at length, consolation added, [saw] him migrate from this life. And ***Athanasius*** writes that ***Antony*** saw the soul of Ammun taken up by Angels. That such things happened at times in the primitive Church, so that they were beheld by the pious and saints, there is no doubt. For what happened in our age — namely in the year 1559 — to Christian, King of Denmark and Norway, is well known, who on the 23rd day of December to his preacher, inquiring how the King was, had said: ***"There has just now been with me one who bade me be of good spirits, and indicated to me that with this new year I should shortly enter into a new life, and my health should be renewed, and my life longer."*** When D. Cornelius the physician had heard this speech of the King, he said, ***"I conjecture it was a dream of everyone."*** ***"By no means,"*** said the King, ***"it was not vain nor like a dream, for that it should certainly be so, he affirmed to me, and the outcome shall show the same."*** On the Kalends of January therefore, from which we auspicate the new year — ***"today,"*** he said, ***"I shall depart"*** — and bidding farewell to all who were present, consoling himself the Queen, and singing the *Canticum Simeonis* ("Mit Fried und Freud"), at length sweetly and placidly, as if dissolved in sleep, he expired, while that verse from Psalm 103 was recited: ***"As a father pitieth his children, so the Lord pitieth them that fear Him."*** Something similar indeed happened to **John Frederick, the Elector-born**, who had many evident signs and testimonies of divine providence — both when he was captured, and when the Sun was of blood-red aspect, sad, obscure, and often pale — not on one day only, but on several days, and not in one place only, but in all Germany, and in France and Britain; and when he returned home, in a most serene sky, with the Sun most rosy and refulgent, and with the widest plausus of all; and when from this life he was to depart. For while a beautiful [tomb] was being prepared for his wife Sibylla, he ordered a place for himself to be reserved at her side, saying he was shortly to follow — and that there had appeared to him a certain one who had said it was best to live with Him, by whom hitherto he had been educated, governed, defended, and preserved. These things concurred with the thing itself. For on the eleventh day after the death of his consort, he fell asleep amid these words: ***"So God loved the world,"*** etc.

**Examples of such Heroes, so pious and illustrious, we ought to observe, often to repeat, and to bring to posterity** — lest that accusation of Isa. 57 may rightly be turned against us: ***"The just perisheth, and no man considereth; and holy men are gathered together, and there is none who understandeth."***¹¹³ᵉ

#### The Cult of Angels — Not by Invocation, but by Holy Life

**Let these things said of the offices of the good Angels suffice. Now on the cult by which the Angels are to be pursued, only this is to be added:** that we must strive with all our forces, ***lest by any offences and shameless licentiousness of sinning we alienate from ourselves so faithful guardians and so loving pedagogues.*** Truly one has sung:

> ***"So let the whole day pass by,***
> ***That neither lying tongue, nor hand,***
> ***Nor slippery eyes may sin,***
> ***Lest guilt defile the body.***
> ***The Watcher stands above,***
> ***Who all our days***
> ***Beholds our actions***
> ***From first light unto the evening.***
> ***This is the witness, this the arbiter,***
> ***This one beholds whatever is,***
> ***Whatever the human mind conceives.***
> ***No one deceives this Judge."***

**And well known is what is wont to be reported to children** — of the Angels who stood by, as often as speeches concerning pious things were held; and of the demons and most filthy swine who wallowed by, as often as anything of base things was uttered. For by drunkenness, blasphemies, and impurity of morals and life, the good Angels are driven away. Let us therefore be pious, modest, sober, chaste — that we may retain the guardianship of the Angels — and let us be mindful of the accustomed saying: ***"Do not sin — God sees, an Angel stands by, death threatens, the devil accuses, conscience bites, the infernal [torments] torture."***

Paul (1 Cor. 11) commands the woman in public assembly to be covered on the head, that she may testify she is subject to the man — and adds: ***"on account of the Angels"*** — showing that if she will not do it on account of men, yet reverence is owed to the Angels.

**The cult most grateful to the Angels, therefore, is that we live a holy and pure life.** For that they be adored, this they themselves cannot bear (Rev. 19, 22). And it must be thought impious, since it is written: ***"Thou shalt adore the Lord thy God."*** Therefore the Angel in the Apocalypse says: [***"See thou do it not: worship God"***]. And for this cause the Patriarchs — even if they saw several Angels — nevertheless adored ***one*** God in them, and directed their speech to ***one*** Lord, as is manifest from Abraham.

These things concerning the good Angels — that they are, and that they were created by God as good Spirits, and how great in number they are (namely innumerable by our reasoning, as the Epistle to the Hebrews at chapter 12 names ***myriads of Angels***; the same does the Epistle of Jude, Rev. 5; and Luke 2 mentions ***the multitude of the heavenly host***); item, what are their offices — namely, that they may guard the true doctrine against all lies and against heresies, and that they may be legates of God, and that they may defend the Church against tyrants and the furies of the world, and that they may guard the life and vocation of individuals, and that they may pray for the Church and for its members, and that they may be present with us in the agony, and transport our souls to the blessed place, and finally that in the resurrection of men they may adorn the coming of Christ and gather the elect (as it is written Matt. 24 and 25; Luke 9; and in the Epistle of Jude) — these things concerning the good Angels we have wished to say in this place.¹¹³ᶠ
